17:["$","$L23",null,{"exam":{"id":"opsc-ocs","name":"OPSC Odisha Civil Services Examination","shortName":"OPSC OCS","authority":"Odisha Public Service Commission","categoryId":"state-civil-services","verticalId":"opsc","active":true,"questionTypes":"$0:t:t:d:r:props:children:1:props:children:1:props:children:1:props:exams:0:questionTypes","freeTier":{"openTopicsPerUnit":2,"practicePerDay":25,"mocksPerMonth":2,"mainsPerMonth":3,"currentAffairsEditions":1,"consultRequests":0},"freeTopicIds":["polity.historical-background","polity.making-of-the-constitution","polity.president","polity.prime-minister-and-council","polity.governor","polity.panchayati-raj","history.harappan-civilisation","history.british-colonial-rule","history.gandhi","aptitude.logical-reasoning"],"features":["dashboard","announcements","study","search","current-affairs","downloads","practice","quiz","flashcards","simulator","revision","mains-answer","assistant","consult","pricing"],"officialUrl":"https://opsc.gov.in/","description":"Odisha state civil services examination, with Odisha-specific history, culture and governance as a first-class collection.","region":"Odisha","versionLabel":"Next notification","needsVerification":true,"papers":[{"id":"prelims-gs1","name":"Prelims — General Studies Paper I","stage":"Prelims","durationMinutes":120,"marking":"$0:t:t:d:r:props:children:1:props:children:1:props:children:1:props:exams:0:papers:0:marking"}]},"children":["$","$L14",null,{"parallelRouterKey":"children","error":"$undefined","errorStyles":"$undefined","errorScripts":"$undefined","template":["$","$L15",null,{}],"templateStyles":"$undefined","templateScripts":"$undefined","notFound":"$undefined","forbidden":"$undefined","unauthorized":"$undefined"}]}]
28:T4b2,The Battle of Plassey in 1757 marks the beginning of Company dominance in Bengal, and the grant of Diwani in 1765 gave the Company a revenue role in Bengal, Bihar and Orissa. Company rule continued for about a century after Plassey. The Government of India Act of 1858 transferred the government of India from the Company to the Crown. Crown rule then lasted from 1858 to 1947. The syllabus node History of India and Indian National Movement covers this whole span, and the Mains grouping under Indian Heritage and Culture, History and Geography of the World and the Society expects the candidate to discuss structure and consequences rather than memorise isolated battles.

The ordering matters. Plassey precedes Diwani, Diwani precedes 1858, and Crown rule follows 1858 until 1947. An option that places the Crown takeover before Plassey, or treats 1858 as the start of British rule, reverses the sequence and is incorrect. The Company phase was not absent rule: it was commercial and territorial power exercised by a chartered company, while the Crown phase was direct state power exercised in the name of the sovereign. Keeping the agent of rule distinct prevents a standard assertion-reason error.29:T409,The clearest Odisha-specific anchor in the official OPSC material for this topic is the revolt of Jayi Rajaguru and the Paiks of Odisha against the British East India Company in 1804. Rajaguru, described in the material as royal preceptor to Mukundadeva II, mobilised the Paiks, and he was executed on 6 December 1806 at Midnapore. The sequence is fixed: preceptor role, mobilisation of the Paiks, revolt in 1804, execution in 1806. Questions that place the revolt in the nineteenth-century Crown phase, or that attribute it to a revolt against the British Crown rather than the Company, are incorrect because 1804 falls squarely in the Company phase.

British colonial administration in Odisha in the Madras Presidency context forms part of the same Company-era picture. The Odisha anchor therefore does double duty: it tests a regional fact and it tests phase awareness. A candidate who remembers only Plassey and 1858 but misses the 1804 revolt will lose the Odisha-linked question that the official paper uses to test this topic.2a:T47c,Gandhi's rise within the Congress was not a matter of a single speech. He presided over the Belgaum session of the Indian National Congress in 1924, an office held earlier by Badruddin Tyabji as the first Muslim Congress President in 1887 and later by Annie Besant as the first Indian woman Congress President in 1925. These presidency facts appear together in the 2025 OPSC Prelims match-the-columns item, which expects the learner to align leaders with years and firsts. The institutional point matters: Gandhi initiated campaigns, but the Congress Working Committee shared command. Civil Disobedience was authorised by the Working Committee, with Gandhi as the initiator, on 16 February 1930. Treating Gandhi as acting entirely alone misses how the Congress organisation sustained volunteers, funds and local committees.

The syllabus node for this topic is History of India and Indian National Movement, and the Mains grouping places the Gandhian movement under Indian Heritage and Culture, History and Geography of the World and the Society. For Prelims, the emphasis is factual: what was launched, when, by whom authorised, and what followed.2b:T491,The mature Harappan phase, conventionally dated to about 2600-1900 BCE, is the period in which the largest cities and the most standardised town planning appear. The civilisation as a whole had earlier and later phases, but Prelims questions usually test the mature dates rather than the full sequence. The name Harappan comes from the early identification at Harappa in 1826, yet the culture is now understood through many sites, including Mohenjo-Daro, Dholavira and Lothal. Stating that the civilisation is named after Mohenjo-Daro, or that it consists of one site only, is incorrect. Harappa gives the name, Mohenjo-Daro gives the best-documented example.

The extent, with more than a thousand sites over about one million square kilometres, explains why the Harappan world produced shared weights, bricks and town forms over a large area. Odisha lay on the periphery of this extent. The Mahanadi valley sites and trade links with Mesopotamia provide the regional connection, while the core of the civilisation lay farther west and north. Questions on periphery versus core test whether the learner can locate Odisha correctly instead of treating it as the centre.2c:T422,Mohenjo-Daro was the largest city of the Harappan civilisation. Excavations began in 1922, and UNESCO inscribed the Archaeological Ruins at Moenjo-Daro as World Heritage in 1980 under criteria including its testimony to a lost civilisation and its exceptional example of a settlement tradition. The city was built extensively of unbaked brick and arranged on a planned, grid layout. Prominent features recorded for the city include a public bath, an elaborate drainage system, and a structure traditionally described as a granary. The presence of covered drains, wells and streets in regular alignment is the textbook evidence for urban planning in the Harappan world.

For Prelims, keep the attributes together: largest city, unbaked brick, grid layout, public baths, drainage system, granary, excavation from 1922, UNESCO inscription in 1980. Options that change the brick to baked brick only, move the UNESCO year to the nineteenth century, or describe Mohenjo-Daro as unplanned are incorrect because they contradict the planned-city evidence at the site.2d:T5e6,Statement questions ask which of the listed statements are correct, or which conclusion follows from the passage. The distinction is the central trap. Correct refers to consistency with the given material. Follows refers to logical consequence: the conclusion must be unavoidable given the premises, not merely plausible or generally true. A conclusion that adds a new fact, reverses a cause, or generalises beyond the scope of the passage does not follow even when it sounds reasonable.

A reliable method is to mark each statement as supported, contradicted, or unsupported. Supported statements can be worded almost verbatim from the passage. Contradicted statements conflict with a clear premise. Unsupported statements may be true elsewhere but have no basis in the passage. Only supported statements count as correct, and only unavoidable consequences count as following. Combining statements I and II is permitted only when each is independently supported and the combination does not add an assumption. Over-assuming is the habit to suppress.

Example pattern: a passage states that a library opens on alternate days and that membership requires a local address. The statement that the library opens daily is contradicted, the statement that non-local visitors cannot hold membership is unsupported without a further premise, and the statement that membership is not automatic on entry follows only if the passage makes address a requirement without exception. The pattern teaches restraint, not speed.2e:T590,Series questions present terms in order and ask for the next term or the missing term. The first step is to test the simplest rule that fits the visible terms: differences between consecutive terms, multiplication by a growing factor, alternation between two interleaved series, or digit operations within terms. If the simplest rule fails on a middle term, abandon it quickly rather than forcing later terms to fit. Interleaved series, where odd-positioned and even-positioned terms follow separate rules, are common in Paper II.

Analogy and relationship tasks test mapping. In an analogy, the relation between the first pair must match the relation between the second pair, not merely share a topic. In a relationship task, the categories may be nested, disjoint, or partially overlapping. A Venn-type reading is often decisive: if one category is fully inside another, a third category that overlaps both cannot be drawn as fully separate. Candidates who assign each term its everyday meaning without checking the relation will select a distracting option that shares vocabulary but not structure.

Example pattern: the sequence 2, 6, 12, 20, 30 follows adding 4, 6, 8, 10, so the next term is 42. The distractor 44 arises from assuming constant addition of 6, while 36 arises from halving the growth. The correct choice follows the growth of differences, whereas the distractors follow plausible but inconsistent rules.2f:T464,Analytical puzzles give a set of conditions about persons, dates, places or arrangements and ask for the only arrangement that satisfies all of them. The method is to list the conditions, place the most restrictive condition first, and eliminate arrangements that violate any single condition. Care with data sufficiency and relationship logic is essential: avoid assuming that a missing detail is irrelevant, and avoid treating a possible arrangement as the only arrangement. Blood relation and direction tasks test the same discipline on family trees and positions. A symbol map fixed at the start prevents mid-question reinterpretation of the same sign.

Coding tasks test consistent substitution. The learner must infer the code for a word or number from given examples and apply the same rule without adding an extra step. Letter-position shifts, reverse alphabets, and digit sums are frequent families, but the rule must be inferred from the examples rather than borrowed from memory of a different code. If two rules fit the examples, the option set usually breaks the tie: only one rule will produce an offered code.30:T55a,The **Regulating Act of 1773** was Parliament's first attempt to supervise the Company, and it accepted that the Company had political as well as trading functions. It turned the Governor of Bengal into the Governor-General of Bengal, gave him an executive council of four, and placed the Bombay and Madras presidencies under him. Warren Hastings was the first holder. It also provided for a Supreme Court at Calcutta, set up in 1774 with a chief justice and three judges, and barred Company servants from private trade and from taking gifts.

The **Amending Act of 1781** (the Act of Settlement) repaired gaps in that scheme. Official acts of the Governor-General, his council and Company servants were taken out of the Supreme Court's reach, as were revenue matters, and the Court was told to apply the personal law of the defendant.

**Pitt's India Act of 1784** split the Company's commercial work from its political work. The Court of Directors kept commerce; a new **Board of Control** in Britain supervised civil, military and revenue government. This arrangement is remembered as **double government**, and the Act was also the first to describe the Company's lands as "British possessions in India". The **Act of 1786** then met Lord Cornwallis's conditions for taking office: he could override his council in special cases and would also be Commander-in-Chief.31:T5b1,| Act | What changed |
|---|---|
| Charter Act 1793 | Overriding power over the council extended to all later Governors-General and Governors; trade monopoly renewed for twenty years; Board of Control salaries charged to Indian revenues |
| Charter Act 1813 | Company's general trade monopoly ended (tea and China trade kept); missionaries allowed; western education to be promoted; local governments could tax |
| Charter Act 1833 | Governor-General of Bengal became **Governor-General of India** (Lord William Bentinck first); all legislative power centralised in him; the Company became a purely administrative body; laws now called Acts, not Regulations |
| Charter Act 1853 | Legislative and executive functions of the council separated for the first time; six legislative councillors added, forming a central legislative council; open competition for the covenanted civil service; local representation from four provincial governments |

The 1833 Act is the high point of **centralisation**: Bombay and Madras lost their law-making power. It also proposed open competition for Company posts without excluding Indians, but the Court of Directors blocked it. That idea was revived in 1853, when the covenanted civil service was opened to competitive entry and the Macaulay Committee was set up in 1854. Unlike earlier charters, the 1853 Act renewed the Company's rule without a fixed term, which signalled that Parliament could end it whenever it chose.32:T633,The **Government of India Act of 1858**, passed after the revolt of 1857, wound up the East India Company and transferred its territories, powers and revenues to the Crown. The Governor-General was now also styled **Viceroy**, the Crown's direct representative; Lord Canning was the first. Double government ended: the Board of Control and the Court of Directors were abolished, and a **Secretary of State for India**, a British cabinet member answerable to Parliament, took full control, assisted by an advisory Council of India of fifteen members. Observers note that the Act changed who controlled India from London far more than how India was governed on the ground.

The **Indian Councils Act of 1861** began associating Indians with law-making. The Viceroy could nominate Indians as non-official members, and in 1862 Lord Canning nominated the Raja of Benaras, the Maharaja of Patiala and Sir Dinkar Rao. The Act reversed the trend of 1833 by restoring legislative powers to Bombay and Madras, provided for new councils in Bengal, the North-Western Provinces and Punjab, gave legal recognition to Canning's **portfolio system**, and allowed the Viceroy to issue ordinances valid for six months in an emergency.

The **Indian Councils Act of 1892** enlarged the councils while keeping an official majority, let members discuss the budget and question the executive, and allowed some non-official seats to be filled on the recommendation of bodies such as universities, chambers of commerce and district boards. The word "election" was avoided, but this was election in all but name.33:T6fa,The **Indian Councils Act of 1909** (Morley–Minto Reforms) raised the Central Legislative Council from 16 to 60 members, allowed non-official majorities in the provincial councils (but not at the Centre), and let members ask supplementary questions and move resolutions on the budget. For the first time an Indian joined the Viceroy's executive council: **Satyendra Prasad Sinha**, as Law Member. Most controversially it created **separate electorates** for Muslims, so that Muslim members were chosen only by Muslim voters; Lord Minto is therefore called the father of communal electorate.

The **Government of India Act of 1919** (Montagu–Chelmsford Reforms) followed the declaration of 20 August 1917 that responsible government was the goal, and took effect in 1921.

- Subjects were split into **central and provincial** lists through the Devolution Rules, though the overall structure stayed unitary.
- Provincial subjects were divided into **transferred** (such as education, health and local government, run by ministers answerable to the legislature) and **reserved** (such as police, justice, land revenue and finance, run by the Governor and his executive council). This was **dyarchy**, and it largely failed.
- **Bicameralism and direct elections** appeared at the Centre: a Council of State and a Legislative Assembly.
- Three of the six members of the Viceroy's executive council, other than the Commander-in-Chief, had to be Indians.
- Separate electorates were extended to Sikhs, Indian Christians, Anglo-Indians and Europeans; franchise was limited by property, tax and education.
- A High Commissioner for India in London, a public service commission (the Central Public Service Commission came in 1926), separate provincial budgets, and a review after ten years.34:T55b,A long statute of 321 sections and 10 schedules, the 1935 Act is the single largest source of the administrative structure the Constitution later adopted.

| Feature | Detail |
|---|---|
| All-India Federation | Provinces and princely states as units; three lists — Federal (59 items), Provincial (54), Concurrent (36); residuary powers with the Governor-General. Never came into being because the states did not join |
| Provincial autonomy | Dyarchy ended in the provinces; Governors to act on the advice of ministers responsible to the legislature. Worked from 1937 to 1939 |
| Dyarchy at the Centre | Federal subjects split into reserved and transferred — never implemented |
| Bicameral provinces | Six of eleven: Bengal, Bombay, Madras, Bihar, Assam, United Provinces |
| Franchise | About 14 per cent of the population could vote; separate representation also for women and labour |
| New institutions | Reserve Bank of India; Federal, Provincial and Joint Public Service Commissions; Federal Court (1937); Federal Railway Authority; Auditor-General |
| Territorial changes | Burma separated; new provinces of **Orissa** and Sind created |

The Council of India of 1858 was abolished, and the Secretary of State got a team of advisers instead. For an Odisha aspirant, the creation of Orissa as a separate province under this Act is a natural one-mark question.35:T61c,Prime Minister Clement Attlee announced on 20 February 1947 that British rule would end by June 1948. The Mountbatten Plan of 3 June 1947, accepted by the Congress and the Muslim League, brought partition forward, and the **Indian Independence Act** gave it legal effect.

- India became independent from 15 August 1947, partitioned into the dominions of India and Pakistan, each free to leave the Commonwealth.
- The Viceroy's office ended; each dominion got a Governor-General appointed on the advice of its own cabinet, and the Secretary of State for India was abolished.
- Each Constituent Assembly could frame any constitution and repeal any British law, including this Act, and could legislate meanwhile. Until new constitutions came, the 1935 Act governed both dominions, with modifications.
- British paramountcy over the princely states lapsed; they could join India, join Pakistan or stay independent.
- The Governor-General and provincial Governors became constitutional heads acting on ministerial advice; the British monarch lost the veto and the title Emperor of India.

Lord Mountbatten became the first Governor-General of the Dominion of India and swore in Jawaharlal Nehru as Prime Minister. The Constituent Assembly formed in 1946 also served as the dominion's Parliament. In the **Interim Government of 1946** the members sat as the Viceroy's executive council, with Nehru as its vice-president (External Affairs) and Sardar Patel holding Home; in the **first cabinet of 1947**, Dr B. R. Ambedkar held Law and Maulana Abul Kalam Azad Education.36:T741,The Assembly was set up in **November 1946** on the lines of the Cabinet Mission Plan. Its design had five elements.

| Element | Rule |
|---|---|
| Total strength | **389** — 296 for British India and 93 for princely states |
| British India split | 292 from the eleven Governors' provinces + 4 from the four Chief Commissioners' provinces (one each: Delhi, Ajmer-Merwara, Coorg, British Baluchistan) |
| Basis of allocation | Seats in proportion to population, roughly one per million |
| Communities | Each province's seats shared among Muslims, Sikhs and General in proportion to their population |
| Method | Members of each community in the provincial assembly elected their own representatives by proportional representation through the single transferable vote; princely-state members were nominated by their rulers |

So the Assembly was **partly elected and partly nominated**, and even the elected part was chosen **indirectly** by provincial legislators who had themselves been elected on the narrow franchise of the 1935 Act.

Elections for the 296 British-Indian seats took place in **July–August 1946**. The Congress won **208**, the Muslim League **73**, and small groups and independents **15**. The princely states kept away, so their 93 seats lay empty at first. Despite the indirect method, the Assembly drew in Hindus, Muslims, Sikhs, Parsis, Anglo-Indians, Indian Christians, Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes, including women from these groups — **15 women** in all. Almost every leading figure of the time was a member; the notable exception was Mahatma Gandhi. Among the women, **Malati Chowdhury** represented Orissa.

| Community (1946) | Members |
|---|---|
| Hindus | 163 |
| Muslims | 80 |
| Scheduled Castes | 31 |
| Indian Christians | 6 |
| Backward Tribes | 6 |
| Sikhs | 4 |
| Anglo-Indians | 3 |
| Parsees | 3 |37:T546,The Assembly first met on **9 December 1946**. The Muslim League boycotted it and pressed for Pakistan, so only 207 members attended. In line with French practice, the oldest member, **Dr Sachchidananda Sinha**, presided as temporary Chairman.

| Date | Office-bearer |
|---|---|
| 11 December 1946 | **Dr Rajendra Prasad** elected permanent Chairman (President) |
| 25 January 1947 | H. C. Mukherjee elected Vice-President |
| 16 July 1947 | V. T. Krishnamachari elected as a second Vice-President |

On **13 December 1946** Nehru moved the **Objectives Resolution**, which set out the philosophy of the future constitution. Its main ideas were these: India would be an independent sovereign republic; British India, the princely states and other willing territories would form a Union; the units would be autonomous and hold residuary powers except those given to the Union; all authority would flow from the people; social, economic and political justice, equality of status and opportunity and a set of freedoms would be guaranteed, subject to law and public morality; minorities, backward and tribal areas and depressed classes would get safeguards; territorial integrity would be preserved; and India would contribute to world peace. The Assembly adopted it unanimously on **22 January 1947**, and a modified form of it became the **Preamble**.38:T722,Princely-state members began to trickle in: by 28 April 1947 six states (Baroda, Bikaner, Jaipur, Patiala, Rewa, Udaipur) were represented, and after the Mountbatten Plan of 3 June 1947 most others followed, as did League members from the Indian side. The **Indian Independence Act of 1947** then changed the Assembly's position in three ways.

- **Sovereign body:** it could frame whatever constitution it chose and could repeal or alter any British law applying to India.
- **Legislature too:** it became the Dominion Legislature, the first Parliament of free India, meeting on separate days for each role. Rajendra Prasad presided over constitution-making; **G. V. Mavalankar** presided over law-making (he was elected its Speaker when it first sat as a legislature on 17 November 1947). Both roles ran until 26 November 1949.
- **Smaller house:** League members from areas going to Pakistan left, so strength fell from 389 to **299** — the provinces from 296 to 229 and the princely states from 93 to 70. Pakistan got its own Constituent Assembly.

Beyond drafting and legislating, the Assembly also performed several symbolic acts.

| Date | Act |
|---|---|
| 22 July 1947 | Adopted the national flag |
| May 1949 | Ratified India's Commonwealth membership |
| 24 January 1950 | Adopted the national anthem and the national song; elected Rajendra Prasad first President |

The work took **11 sessions** across 2 years, 11 months and 18 days, drew on the constitutions of about 60 countries, devoted 114 days to the draft, and cost about ₹64 lakh. The final session was on 24 January 1950, when members signed the document. The Assembly then carried on as the **provisional Parliament** from 26 January 1950 until the first elected Parliament, formed after the 1951–52 general elections, came into being in May 1952.39:T644,The Assembly divided its work among eight major committees and many minor ones. The chairmen are a favourite match-the-pair topic.

| Major committee | Chairman |
|---|---|
| Union Powers Committee | Jawaharlal Nehru |
| Union Constitution Committee | Jawaharlal Nehru |
| States Committee (negotiating with the states) | Jawaharlal Nehru |
| Provincial Constitution Committee | Sardar Patel |
| Advisory Committee on Fundamental Rights, Minorities and Tribal and Excluded Areas | Sardar Patel |
| Drafting Committee | Dr B. R. Ambedkar |
| Rules of Procedure Committee | Dr Rajendra Prasad |
| Steering Committee | Dr Rajendra Prasad |

Patel's Advisory Committee worked through sub-committees: Fundamental Rights (**J. B. Kripalani**), Minorities (**H. C. Mukherjee**), North-East Frontier Tribal Areas and Assam excluded areas (**Gopinath Bardoloi**), other excluded and partially excluded areas (**A. V. Thakkar**), and a North-West Frontier sub-committee that never functioned because that region went to Pakistan.

Among the minor bodies, Rajendra Prasad also led the Finance and Staff Committee and the ad hoc committee on the National Flag; Nehru led the special committee to examine the draft; Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar headed the Credentials Committee; K. M. Munshi the Order of Business Committee; B. Pattabhi Sitaramayya the House Committee; and G. V. Mavalankar the committee on the Assembly's functions. Some chairs were outsiders: S. Varadachari (Supreme Court and Citizenship ad hoc committees), Nalini Ranjan Sarkar (financial provisions) and S. K. Dar (Linguistic Provinces Commission).3a:T721,| Stage | Dates | What happened |
|---|---|---|
| First reading | 4–9 November 1948 | Ambedkar introduced the final draft; five days of general debate |
| Second reading | 15 November 1948 – 17 October 1949 | Clause-by-clause scrutiny; 7,653 amendments tabled, 2,473 actually discussed |
| Third reading | from 14 November 1949 | Ambedkar's motion to pass the Constitution "as settled by the Assembly" |
| Adoption | 26 November 1949 | Motion carried; 284 of the 299 members present signed |

The date of adoption, **26 November 1949**, is the one recorded in the Preamble. As adopted, the Constitution had a Preamble, **395 Articles and 8 Schedules**; the Preamble itself was passed last. Ambedkar, then Law Minister, steered the draft through the House and is honoured as the **Father** and **chief architect** of the Constitution. Since a government decision of 19 November 2015, 26 November is observed every year as **Constitution Day (Samvidhan Divas)**.

Enforcement came in two steps. A small group of provisions — on citizenship, elections, the provisional Parliament, temporary and transitional matters and the short title (Articles 5–9, 60, 324, 366, 367, 379, 380, 388, 391, 392 and 393) — took effect on 26 November 1949 itself. The rest came into force on **26 January 1950**, the "date of commencement", now Republic Day. That date was picked because on 26 January 1930 the country had celebrated **Purna Swaraj** day following the Lahore Congress of December 1929. On commencement, the Indian Independence Act 1947 and the Government of India Act 1935 (with its amending Acts) were repealed, though the Abolition of Privy Council Jurisdiction Act 1949 was kept alive.

| Article | Subject |
|---|---|
| 393 | Short title |
| 394 | Commencement |
| 394A | Authoritative Hindi text |
| 395 | Repeals |3b:T46c,The word comes from the Latin **constituere**, "to establish" or "to set up". In current usage a constitution is the set of principles that lays down how a government is organised and how it works, and what rights and duties link the government with the people. It goes by many labels: the fundamental law of the land, the supreme or basic law, the instrument of government, the basic structure of the polity, or the **grundnorm** of the country.

Scholars stress different aspects of the idea.

| Scholar | Emphasis of the definition |
|---|---|
| Gilchrist | The rules that organise government, distribute power among its organs and fix the principles for using that power |
| Gettell | The basic principles that decide the form of the state, including how sovereign power is shared, how functions are exercised and how government relates to the governed |
| K. C. Wheare | The entire system of government — the body of rules that create and regulate it |
| Wade and Phillips | A document with special legal sanctity that sets out the framework and main functions of the organs of the state and the principles they must follow |3c:T460,These two classifications overlap heavily — the source itself treats the distinction as roughly the same.

| Basis | Type | Nature | Also called | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Origin | **Evolved** | Grows slowly through conventions, practices, principles and court rulings | Cumulative | Britain |
| Origin | **Enacted** | Deliberately framed by a constituent assembly or council, or issued by a king or parliament, as a document or set of documents | Conventional | USA, India |
| Form | **Written** | Provisions gathered in a document or series of documents, consciously adopted by a constituent body | Documentary, codified | USA, Canada, Japan, France, India |
| Form | **Unwritten** | Most rules found outside any single text — in conventions, charters, statutes and judgments; a product of history | Non-documentary, uncodified | UK, New Zealand, Israel |

Do not confuse the word "conventional" here. An enacted constitution is called conventional because a convention (an assembly) made it, not because it rests on conventions — the British evolved constitution is the one that relies on conventions.3d:T656,Having a constitution is not the same as having **constitutionalism**. A dictatorship may have a constitutional document while its rulers remain unchecked. Constitutionalism means, at its heart, **limited government** — the opposite of arbitrary power. A country has it only when its constitution disperses power rather than concentrating it and places further restraints on those who hold it. Even power handed over by a constitutional text, if it is arbitrary, defeats the idea. Constitutional government should therefore be democratic government, ruled by law rather than by despotic, authoritarian or totalitarian will; some writers call such limited government "civilised" government.

| Thinker | Core idea |
|---|---|
| Carl Friedrich | A system of effective checks on government action — rules of fair play that make government responsible |
| J. S. Roucek | Essentially limited government, the reverse of rule by the unrestrained will of rulers |
| K. C. Wheare | Rule-based government limited by the constitution's terms, not merely by what rulers wish or are able to do |
| S. P. Ybema | Government counts as constitutional only when rulers are bound by rules that limit their power |

**Louis Henkin** listed eight elements of constitutionalism:

| # | Element |
|---|---|
| 1 | Popular sovereignty |
| 2 | Rule of law |
| 3 | Democratic, responsible and accountable government |
| 4 | Separation of powers with checks and balances |
| 5 | Independent judiciary |
| 6 | Civilian control of the armed forces |
| 7 | Police bound by law and subject to judicial control |
| 8 | Respect for individual rights |3e:T7ab,No written constitution in the world is longer. Compare the original and present shape:

| | Original (1949) | Present |
|---|---|---|
| Articles | 395 | about 470 |
| Parts | 22 | 25 |
| Schedules | 8 | 12 |

Amendments since 1951 have dropped about 20 Articles and one Part (Part VII, on Part-B states, deleted by the 7th Amendment of 1956) and added about 95 Articles, four Parts — **IV-A** and **XIV-A** (42nd Amendment), **IX-A** (74th) and **IX-B** (97th) — and four Schedules (9th to 12th). For comparison, the US Constitution began with just 7 Articles.

Four factors explain the bulk: the size and diversity of the country; the historical pull of the long 1935 Act; the use of a **single constitution for both the Centre and the states**; and the large number of lawyers in the Constituent Assembly. The document therefore contains administrative detail and matters that other democracies leave to ordinary law or convention.

Until 2019, Jammu and Kashmir had its own constitution and special status under Article 370. The Constitution (Application to Jammu and Kashmir) Order, 2019, replaced the 1954 Order and applied the whole Constitution there, and the Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act, 2019, split the state into two Union territories — Jammu & Kashmir, and Ladakh.

| Schedule | Content (selected) |
|---|---|
| 7th | Union List (now 98 subjects, originally 97), State List (59, originally 66), Concurrent List (52, originally 47) |
| 8th | Languages: 14 at first, now 22 — Sindhi (21st Amdt, 1967); Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali (71st, 1992); Bodo, Dogri, Maithili, Santhali (92nd, 2003); Oriya renamed **Odia** (96th, 2011) |
| 9th | Land-reform and other laws shielded from fundamental-rights challenge (1st Amdt, 1951); laws added after 24 April 1973 open to judicial review |
| 10th | Anti-defection (52nd Amdt, 1985) |
| 11th | Panchayats — 29 matters (73rd Amdt, 1992) |
| 12th | Municipalities — 18 matters (74th Amdt, 1992) |3f:T6e9,Ambedkar said the framers had "ransacked" all the known constitutions of the world. A useful three-way split: the **structural** part came largely from the 1935 Act; the **philosophical** part (Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles) from the American and Irish constitutions respectively; and the **political** part (cabinet government and executive–legislature relations) from Britain. The 1935 Act was the single biggest material source — roughly 250 of its provisions were taken over, and more than half the Constitution closely resembles it.

| Source | Features borrowed |
|---|---|
| Government of India Act, 1935 | Federal scheme, office of Governor, judiciary, public service commissions, emergency provisions, Supreme Court's advisory jurisdiction, administrative details |
| Britain | Parliamentary government, rule of law, legislative procedure, single citizenship, cabinet system, prerogative writs, parliamentary privileges, bicameralism |
| USA | Fundamental rights, independent judiciary, judicial review, impeachment of the President, removal of judges, office of Vice-President |
| Ireland | Directive Principles, nomination of members to the Rajya Sabha, method of electing the President |
| Canada | Federation with a strong Centre, residuary powers with the Centre, Centre's appointment of Governors |
| Australia | Concurrent List, freedom of trade and commerce, joint sitting of the two Houses |
| Weimar Germany | Suspension of Fundamental Rights during emergency |
| USSR | Fundamental duties; social, economic and political justice in the Preamble |
| France | Republic; liberty, equality and fraternity in the Preamble |
| South Africa | Amendment procedure; election of Rajya Sabha members |
| Japan | Procedure established by law |40:T4dc,India preferred the British parliamentary model, which rests on co-operation between legislature and executive, to the American presidential model, which rests on their separation. Also called the Westminster model, responsible government or cabinet government, it operates at both the Centre and in the states. Its features in India are: a nominal and a real executive; rule by the majority party; collective responsibility of the executive to the legislature; ministers drawn from the legislature; leadership by the Prime Minister or Chief Minister; and the power to dissolve the lower House. The PM's dominance is such that some call it **"Prime Ministerial government"**.

Two differences from Britain matter: the Indian Parliament is **not sovereign**, and India has an **elected head of state** (a republic), whereas Britain has a hereditary monarch.

On the other side, Indian judicial review is narrower than American, because Article 21 uses **"procedure established by law"** rather than the US **"due process of law"**. So the framers struck a middle course between British **parliamentary sovereignty** and American **judicial supremacy**: the Supreme Court can strike down laws, while Parliament can amend most of the Constitution.41:T79b,| Feature | Fundamental Rights (Part III) | Directive Principles (Part IV) | Fundamental Duties (Part IV-A) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Purpose | Political democracy; check on executive tyranny and arbitrary laws | Social and economic democracy; a welfare state | Remind citizens of obligations to country, society and fellow citizens |
| Justiciable? | Yes — enforceable through writs | No | No |
| Origin | Original | Original; Ambedkar called them a "novel feature" | Added by the 42nd Amdt (1976) on the Swaran Singh Committee's advice; one more by the 86th Amdt (2002) |

There are now **six** Fundamental Rights: equality (Arts 14–18), freedom (19–22), against exploitation (23–24), freedom of religion (25–28), cultural and educational rights (29–30) and constitutional remedies (32). There were seven originally; the 44th Amendment of 1978 removed the right to property (Art 31) and made it a legal right under **Article 300-A** in Part XII. An aggrieved person can go straight to the Supreme Court, which can issue habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, certiorari and quo warranto. The rights are not absolute: they carry reasonable restrictions, can be curtailed by a constitutional amendment, and can be suspended in a National Emergency — except **Articles 20 and 21**.

The Directive Principles fall into socialistic, Gandhian and liberal-intellectual groups. Though courts cannot enforce them, the Constitution calls them fundamental in governance and makes it the state's duty to apply them in law-making; their real sanction is public opinion. In **Minerva Mills (1980)** the Court held that the Constitution rests on a balance between the Rights and the Directives.

Part IV-A has a single Article, **51-A**, listing **eleven** duties — among them respecting the Constitution, flag and anthem, protecting the country's sovereignty, unity and integrity, promoting common brotherhood, and preserving the composite culture.42:T4cf,India has no official religion. The secular character shows in many provisions.

| Provision | Secular content |
|---|---|
| Preamble | "Secular" added by the 42nd Amdt (1976); liberty of belief, faith and worship |
| Arts 14, 15, 16 | Equality before law; no religious discrimination; equal opportunity in public employment |
| Art 25 | Freedom of conscience and to profess, practise and propagate religion |
| Art 26 | Denominations may manage their own religious affairs |
| Art 27 | No compulsion to pay taxes for promoting a religion |
| Art 28 | No religious instruction in fully state-maintained institutions |
| Arts 29, 30 | Protection of language, script and culture; minorities' educational institutions |
| Art 44 | State to try to secure a Uniform Civil Code |

The Western idea of secularism is **negative** — a wall between church and state — which suits a society with one dominant faith poorly. India, being multi-religious, adopts **positive** secularism: equal respect and equal protection for all religions. The Constitution also ended **communal representation** (religion-based seats used under the 1909, 1919 and 1935 Acts), while allowing temporary reservation for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes.43:T404,Elections to the Lok Sabha and state assemblies are held on **universal adult franchise**: every citizen aged 18 or above votes, regardless of caste, race, religion, sex, literacy or wealth. The voting age was cut from 21 to 18 by the **61st Amendment Act of 1988**, effective 1989. Given the country's size, poverty, inequality and illiteracy, this was a bold experiment — many Western countries had extended the vote only slowly (women got it in the USA in 1920, Britain in 1928, France in 1945 and Switzerland only in 1971). It broadens democracy, raises the dignity of ordinary people, upholds equality and gives minorities and weaker sections a voice.

Although India has a dual polity, it has **single citizenship**. An American is a citizen of both the USA and a state, with two sets of rights; an Indian has one citizenship and the same civil and political rights everywhere in the country. Riots and caste, linguistic and ethnic conflicts show, however, that the goal of a fully integrated nation is still unfinished.44:T555,Besides the three organs of government, the Constitution creates independent bodies as bulwarks of democracy, protected by security of tenure, fixed service conditions and charged expenditure.

| Body | Role |
|---|---|
| Election Commission | Free and fair elections to Parliament, state legislatures and the offices of President and Vice-President |
| Comptroller and Auditor-General | Audits Central and state accounts; guardian of the public purse |
| Union Public Service Commission | Recruits for the All-India and higher Central services; advises the President on disciplinary matters |
| State Public Service Commissions | Recruit for state services; advise the Governor on discipline |

The emergency provisions aim to protect the country's sovereignty, unity, integrity and security and its democratic order.

| Emergency | Ground | Article |
|---|---|---|
| National | War, external aggression or armed rebellion ("armed rebellion" replaced "internal disturbance" through the 44th Amdt, 1978) | 352 |
| State (President's Rule) | Breakdown of constitutional machinery, or failure to follow Central directions | 356, 365 |
| Financial | Threat to India's financial stability or credit | 360 |

In an emergency the Centre becomes all-powerful, turning the federal structure into a unitary one **without any formal amendment** — a feature unique to India.45:T534,| Criticism | Main voices | Response or note |
|---|---|---|
| A borrowed constitution — a "bag of borrowings", "hotch-potch", "patchwork" | General critics | Ambedkar: so late in history, all constitutions must look alike in essentials; the new element lies in adapting them and removing their faults |
| A carbon copy of the 1935 Act | N. Srinivasan; Ivor Jennings; P. R. Deshmukh (the 1935 Act "with only adult franchise added") | Ambedkar: borrowing is no plagiarism and nobody owns basic constitutional ideas; he regretted only that the borrowed parts were mostly administrative detail |
| Un-Indian or anti-Indian | K. Hanumanthaiya (wanted the veena or sitar, got an English band); Lokanath Misra ("slavish imitation of the west"); Lakshminarayan Sahu (predicted breakdown) | It does not reflect Indian political traditions, critics said |
| Un-Gandhian | K. Hanumanthaiya; T. Prakasam (blamed Ambedkar's distance from the Gandhian movement) | Should have been built on village and district panchayats |
| Elephantine size | Ivor Jennings; H. V. Kamath (linked it to the Assembly's elephant emblem) | Too long, too detailed, with poorly chosen borrowings |
| Lawyers' paradise | Ivor Jennings coined the phrase; H. K. Maheswari; P. R. Deshmukh (too "ponderous", like a law manual) | Legalistic language invites litigation |46:T650,**Sovereign** means that India is not a dependency or dominion of another country. No authority stands above the Indian State, and it manages its internal and external affairs freely. Until the Indian Independence Act of 1947 India was a colony; from 15 August 1947 to 26 January 1950 it was a dominion; it stopped being a dominion on 26 January 1950 by becoming a sovereign republic. Two points are often tested. India's continued membership of the Commonwealth, with the British Crown accepted as its symbolic head, does not dilute sovereignty, because the link is voluntary and can be ended at will. Membership of the United Nations (India joined in 1945) is likewise no limit on sovereignty. As a sovereign State, India may acquire foreign territory or cede its own to another State.

**Socialist** entered the text only in 1976, but the idea was already present through several Directive Principles, so the amendment made explicit what had been implicit. The Congress had resolved at its Avadi session in 1955 to work for a socialistic pattern of society. The Indian model is **democratic socialism**: a mixed economy in which public and private sectors operate together. It differs from state socialism, which nationalises all means of production and ends private property. The Supreme Court has described it as aiming to end poverty, ignorance, disease and inequality of opportunity, and as a blend of Marxism and Gandhism that leans towards the Gandhian side. The economic reforms of 1991 (liberalisation, privatisation, globalisation) are widely seen as having softened the socialist character of the State.47:T7b7,**Secular** also came through the 42nd Amendment. Even before that, the Supreme Court said in 1974 that the framers plainly meant to create a secular State, and that Articles 25 to 28, which protect freedom of religion, show it. India follows the **positive** idea of secularism: every religion, whatever its numbers, has equal standing and receives equal support from the State. States can be grouped by their stance on religion.

| Type of State | Attitude to religion | Example given |
|---|---|---|
| Atheistic | Opposes all religion | – |
| Theocratic | Prefers one religion as the State religion | Pakistan, Sri Lanka |
| Secular | Neutral; no State religion | India, USA |

**Democratic** means that the highest power belongs to the people. Democracy may be direct or indirect. In direct democracy, as in Switzerland, people rule themselves through devices such as **referendum, initiative, recall and plebiscite**. In indirect or representative democracy, elected representatives run the government and make laws; this form may be parliamentary or presidential. India has a representative parliamentary system, where the executive answers to the legislature. Its marks include adult franchise, regular elections, the rule of law, an independent judiciary and non-discrimination.

The Preamble uses the word broadly, covering social and economic democracy as well as political. Dr Ambedkar made the point on 25 November 1949: political democracy cannot last without social democracy beneath it, and liberty, equality and fraternity form a union, so that separating any one from the others defeats the purpose of democracy.

| Device | What it allows |
|---|---|
| Referendum | A proposed law is put to the electorate for a direct vote |
| Initiative | People themselves propose a bill to the legislature |
| Recall | Voters remove a representative before the term ends |
| Plebiscite | Public opinion is sought on an important question, often a territorial dispute |48:T92b,**Justice** has three forms. Social justice means equal treatment without distinction of caste, colour, race, religion or sex, and better conditions for backward classes and women. Economic justice means no discrimination on economic grounds and the narrowing of glaring gaps in wealth, income and property. Social and economic justice together are called **distributive justice**. Political justice means equal political rights, equal access to office and an equal voice in government. The triad is said to be borrowed from the Russian Revolution of 1917.

**Liberty** means freedom from restraint combined with opportunity to develop one's personality. The Preamble lists liberty of thought, expression, belief, faith and worship, which the Fundamental Rights make enforceable in court. It is not licence: it is qualified by limits in the Constitution itself. Liberty, equality and fraternity are said to come from the French Revolution (1789–1799).

**Equality** means no special privileges for any section and adequate opportunity for all. Three dimensions are present.

| Dimension | Provisions |
|---|---|
| Civic | Article 14 (equality before law), 15 (no discrimination on religion, race, caste, sex, place of birth), 16 (public employment), 17 (untouchability abolished), 18 (titles abolished) |
| Political | Article 325 (no one excluded from electoral rolls on grounds of religion, race, caste or sex) and Article 326 (adult suffrage for Lok Sabha and Assembly elections) |
| Economic | Article 39 (Directive Principle): adequate livelihood for men and women alike, equal pay for equal work |

**Fraternity** means a feeling of brotherhood. The Constitution encourages it through single citizenship and through the Fundamental Duty in Article 51A to promote harmony among all people and to give up practices that demean women. The Preamble says fraternity must secure the **dignity of the individual** and the **unity and integrity of the nation**; "integrity" was added in 1976. K. M. Munshi explained dignity as recognising every person as sacred. Unity and integrity cover both psychological and territorial national integration. Article 1 calls India a Union of States, signalling that states cannot secede and that the Union is indestructible. The aim is to overcome communalism, regionalism, casteism, linguism and secessionism.49:T50d,The courts moved through three stages.

| Case | Holding |
|---|---|
| Berubari Union (1960) | The Preamble is a key to the minds of the makers and can help to interpret ambiguous articles, but it is **not** a part of the Constitution |
| Kesavananda Bharati (1973) | Overruled that view: the Preamble **is** part of the Constitution and the whole document is to be read in its light |
| LIC of India (1995) | Again held that it is an integral part |

The Preamble was enacted by the Constituent Assembly like the rest, but only after the other provisions had been passed, so that it would match them. The Assembly's President put the question that the Preamble stand part of the Constitution, and it was adopted, so the present judicial view matches the founders' intent. Two limits remain: the Preamble neither confers power on the legislature nor restricts it, and it is **non-justiciable**, meaning it cannot be enforced in court.

On amendability, the question arose for the first time in Kesavananda Bharati. The Court held that the Preamble can be amended under Article 368 provided the **basic features** are not altered, since the Constitution's edifice rests on the basic elements named in the Preamble. The Preamble has been amended once, in 1976, and that amendment was held valid.4a:T6db,Article 1 calls the country "India, that is, Bharat, a Union of States". It settles two matters. On the name, the Constituent Assembly could not agree between the traditional name (Bharat) and the modern one (India), so it adopted both. On the polity, it chose "Union" even though the structure is federal. Dr Ambedkar gave two reasons: the Indian federation did not arise from an agreement among states, as the American one did, and states have no right to secede. The country is one integral whole divided into states only for administrative convenience.

The territory of India falls into three groups: the territories of the states, the union territories, and any territory the Government of India may acquire in future. The First Schedule lists states and union territories along with their extent. There are now **28 states and 8 union territories**.

| Expression | Includes |
|---|---|
| Union of India | Only the states, which share powers with the Centre |
| Territory of India | States, union territories and acquired territories; the last two are run directly by the Centre |

The provisions on states apply uniformly to all states, but special provisions in Part XXI override them for some, including Maharashtra, Gujarat, Nagaland, Assam, Manipur, Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Sikkim, Mizoram, Arunachal Pradesh, Goa and Karnataka. The Fifth and Sixth Schedules separately govern scheduled and tribal areas.

India, as a sovereign State, can acquire foreign territory by the modes recognised in international law: cession (through treaty, purchase, gift, lease or plebiscite), occupation of land without a recognised ruler, or conquest. Territories acquired since 1950 include Dadra and Nagar Haveli, Goa, Daman and Diu, Puducherry and Sikkim.4b:T88d,**Article 2** lets Parliament admit into the Union, or establish, new states on terms it thinks fit. Admission applies to states already existing elsewhere, while establishment covers states that did not exist before. It concerns territories outside the Union. **Article 3**, by contrast, handles internal readjustment among the existing states.

Under Article 3 Parliament may form a new state by separating territory from a state, by joining two or more states or parts of states, or by joining a territory to part of a state. It may also increase or reduce a state's area, change its boundaries and change its name. A later amendment (the 18th, in 1966) extended this to forming a new state or union territory by joining part of a state or union territory to another.

Two conditions apply. A bill under Article 3 can be introduced only on the **prior recommendation of the President**, and before recommending it the President must send it to the state legislature concerned to state its views within a set period. The views are not binding, even if received in time, and a fresh reference is unnecessary each time the bill is amended in Parliament. For a union territory, no reference to its legislature is needed at all.

**Article 4** states that laws under Articles 2 and 3 are not constitutional amendments under Article 368. They need only a **simple majority** and the ordinary law-making process. They may also amend the First and Fourth Schedules.

| Article | Subject |
|---|---|
| 1 | Name and territory of the Union |
| 2 | Admission or establishment of new states |
| 2A | Sikkim associated with the Union (repealed) |
| 3 | New states and alteration of areas, boundaries, names |
| 4 | These laws are not amendments under Article 368 |

Because Parliament can redraw the map without a state's consent, no state's territorial integrity is guaranteed. This is the basis for the description of India as an "indestructible union of destructible states". The Union can end a state, while states cannot end the Union. In the United States the opposite holds: a state's borders cannot change without its consent, so it is called an indestructible union of indestructible states.4c:T476,In 1960 the President asked the Supreme Court whether Article 3, which lets Parliament reduce a state's area, also lets it hand territory to a foreign country. The question arose from the Government's decision to give part of Berubari Union in West Bengal to Pakistan. The Court said **no**: ceding Indian territory needs a constitutional amendment under Article 368, and the **9th Amendment (1960)** was passed to carry out that transfer. In 1969 the Court added that settling a boundary dispute with another country needs no amendment, since it is done by executive action and involves no cession.

The same logic lies behind the **100th Amendment (2015)**, which gave effect to the land boundary agreement with Bangladesh. India handed over **111 enclaves** and received **51**; adverse possessions were exchanged and a 6.1 km stretch of border was demarcated. The First Schedule entries of four states changed: Assam, West Bengal, Meghalaya and Tripura. The background runs from the Radcliffe Award (1947) through the Bagge Award (1950), the Nehru–Noon Agreement (1958), the 1974 boundary agreement and its protocol of 6 September 2011.4d:T66b,The integration of princely states was seen as a stopgap, and demand grew, especially in the South, for linguistic states. In June 1948 the **Dhar Commission** was appointed; in December 1948 it favoured administrative convenience, not language, which angered many. The Congress then set up the **JVP Committee** (Jawaharlal Nehru, Vallabhbhai Patel, Pattabhi Sitaramayya), which in April 1949 also set language aside for the time being. After the death of Potti Sriramulu following a 56-day fast, the first linguistic state, **Andhra**, was carved from Madras in October 1953.

The **States Reorganisation Commission** was appointed in December 1953 under Fazl Ali, with K. M. Panikkar and H. N. Kunzru as members. Reporting in September 1955, it accepted language as a basis but rejected "one language, one state", putting national unity first. It named four factors: unity and security of the country; linguistic and cultural homogeneity; financial, economic and administrative needs; and the welfare of people in each state and of the nation. It proposed scrapping the four-fold classification and creating 16 states and 3 centrally administered territories.

The **States Reorganisation Act and the 7th Amendment (1956)** ended the difference between Part A and Part B and abolished Part C. The result on **1 November 1956** was 14 states and 6 union territories. Orissa was among the states. Kerala was formed by merging Travancore–Cochin with Malabar and Kasargode; Andhra Pradesh by joining Telugu areas of Hyderabad with Andhra; and several states, such as Madhya Bharat, Vindhya Pradesh and Bhopal, were folded into Madhya Pradesh.4e:T790,| Year | Change |
|---|---|
| 1960 | Bombay divided into Maharashtra and Gujarat (Gujarat the 15th state) |
| 1961 | Dadra and Nagar Haveli became a union territory (10th Amendment) |
| 1962 | Goa, Daman and Diu became a union territory (12th Amendment) after a police action in 1961; Puducherry became one by the 14th Amendment |
| 1963 | Nagaland, the 16th state, carved out of Assam |
| 1966 | Punjab split into Punjab and Haryana (17th state), with Chandigarh as a union territory; Shah Commission |
| 1971 | Himachal Pradesh raised to the 18th state |
| 1972 | Manipur (19th), Tripura (20th) and Meghalaya (21st) became states; Mizoram and Arunachal Pradesh became union territories |
| 1974–75 | Sikkim: associate state by the 35th Amendment (Article 2A, Tenth Schedule), then the 22nd state by the 36th Amendment after a 1975 referendum (Article 371F) |
| 1987 | Mizoram (23rd), Arunachal Pradesh (24th) and Goa (25th) |
| 2000 | Chhattisgarh (26th), Uttarakhand (27th), Jharkhand (28th) |
| 2014 | Telangana, the 29th state, from Andhra Pradesh |
| 2019 | Jammu and Kashmir state split into two union territories, J&K and Ladakh; Dadra and Nagar Haveli merged with Daman and Diu |

Meghalaya first became an autonomous "sub-state" within Assam by the 22nd Amendment (1969). Mizoram became a state after the 1986 peace accord with the Mizo National Front. Telangana's creation made the numbering reach 29, but the Jammu and Kashmir change in 2019 brought the count of states back to 28.

Name changes are also asked: United Provinces became Uttar Pradesh (1950); Madras became Tamil Nadu (1969); Mysore became Karnataka and the Laccadive, Minicoy and Amindivi Islands became Lakshadweep (1973); Delhi became the National Capital Territory (1992, by the 69th Amendment of 1991); Uttaranchal became Uttarakhand and Pondicherry became Puducherry (2006); and **Orissa became Odisha in 2011** by the Orissa (Alteration of Name) Act.4f:T40d,- Articles 1 to 4 sit in Part I; Article 1 calls India a Union of States, not a federation of states.
- Union because states cannot secede and the federation is not a compact of states.
- Now 28 states and 8 union territories; "Territory of India" is wider than "Union of India".
- Article 2 is about states outside the Union; Article 3 about changes inside it.
- Article 3 bills need the President's prior recommendation and a reference to the state legislature, whose views are not binding.
- Article 4: such laws are passed by simple majority and are not Article 368 amendments.
- Berubari (1960): ceding territory needs an amendment; 9th Amendment followed; 100th Amendment (2015) did the same with Bangladesh.
- The 1950 map had Parts A to D (29 units); the 1956 reorganisation left 14 states and 6 union territories.
- The Fazl Ali Commission (1953–55) accepted language but not "one language, one state".
- Telangana (2014) is the 29th state; Jammu and Kashmir became two union territories in 2019; Orissa became Odisha in 2011.50:T4b6,Aliens are of two kinds. **Friendly aliens** belong to countries with cordial relations with India. **Enemy aliens** belong to a country at war with India, and they have fewer rights; for instance, they lack the protection against arrest and detention in Article 22.

Some rights and privileges are reserved for citizens alone.

| Right or privilege | Source |
|---|---|
| Protection from discrimination on grounds of religion, race, caste, sex or place of birth | Article 15 |
| Equal opportunity in public employment | Article 16 |
| Freedoms of speech, assembly, association, movement, residence and profession | Article 19 |
| Cultural and educational rights | Articles 29 and 30 |
| Voting in Lok Sabha and Assembly elections; contesting for Parliament and state legislatures | Representation law |
| Holding office as President, Vice-President, Supreme Court and High Court judge, Governor, Attorney General, Advocate General | Constitution |

Citizens also owe duties, such as paying taxes, respecting the flag and anthem and defending the country. A point often asked: in India both natural-born and naturalised citizens can become President, whereas in the USA only a citizen by birth is eligible.51:T6af,India is federal and has a dual polity of Centre and states, but only **one** citizenship, Indian citizenship; there is no separate state citizenship, and citizens owe allegiance only to the Union. The USA and Australia follow double citizenship, so a person there belongs both to the nation and to a state and holds two sets of rights. That arrangement lets a state favour its own citizens in matters like voting, public office and professions. India avoids this, and the Constitution, like that of Canada, uses single citizenship to foster fraternity and an integrated nation.

There are, however, exceptions to equal treatment across states.

| Exception | Detail |
|---|---|
| Article 16 | Parliament may require residence within a state or union territory for certain jobs. The Public Employment (Requirement as to Residence) Act, 1957 allowed this for non-gazetted posts in Andhra Pradesh, Himachal Pradesh, Manipur and Tripura; it lapsed in 1974, and now Andhra Pradesh and Telangana have such a rule only through Article 371D |
| Article 15 | It bars discrimination on place of birth but not on residence, so a state can offer residents benefits such as concessional fees |
| Article 19 | Movement and residence can be restricted to protect the interests of Scheduled Tribes, keeping outsiders from settling in tribal areas |
| Jammu and Kashmir (until 2019) | The state legislature could define permanent residents and give them special rights in jobs, property, settlement and scholarships, under Article 35A; the 2019 presidential order ended the special status |

Despite single citizenship, communal riots, caste conflicts and linguistic disputes show that national integration is still incomplete.52:T55d,Part II (Articles 5 to 11) is neither permanent nor detailed. It identifies the people who became citizens on **26 January 1950** and does not cover later acquisition or loss. Parliament was authorised to legislate, and it did so through the **Citizenship Act, 1955**, amended several times.

Four categories became citizens at commencement.

| Article | Category | Key conditions |
|---|---|---|
| 5 | Domiciled in India | Born in India, or a parent born in India, or ordinarily resident for five years before commencement |
| 6 | Migrated from Pakistan | Self, parent or grandparent born in undivided India; if migrated before 19 July 1948, ordinary residence since migration; if on or after that date, registration after six months' residence (19 July 1948 is when the permit system began) |
| 7 | Migrated to Pakistan but returned | Moved after 1 March 1947, came back for resettlement, registered after six months' residence |
| 8 | Persons of Indian origin living abroad | Self, parent or grandparent born in undivided India; registered by an Indian diplomatic or consular officer |

Other provisions: a person who voluntarily acquires foreign citizenship is not a citizen (Article 9); existing citizens continue as such subject to Parliament's law (Article 10); and Parliament may regulate acquisition, termination and every other aspect of citizenship (Article 11).53:Tc0a,The Act lists five modes: birth, descent, registration, naturalisation and incorporation of territory. It originally also provided Commonwealth citizenship, which the Citizenship (Amendment) Act of 2003 removed.

**By birth.** The rule has tightened in three stages.

| Born in India | Condition |
|---|---|
| 26 Jan 1950 to 30 Jun 1987 | Citizen regardless of parents' nationality |
| 1 Jul 1987 to 2 Dec 2004 | Citizen if either parent was a citizen at the time of birth |
| From 3 Dec 2004 | Citizen if both parents are citizens, or one is a citizen and the other is not an illegal migrant |

Children of foreign diplomats and of enemy aliens cannot get citizenship by birth.

**By descent.** For a person born abroad, between 26 January 1950 and 9 December 1992 the **father** had to be an Indian citizen; from 10 December 1992 either parent suffices. Since 3 December 2004 the birth must be registered at an Indian consulate within a year (or later with Central Government permission), with an undertaking that a minor holds no other passport. A minor who is a citizen by descent and also holds another nationality ceases to be an Indian citizen unless the other citizenship is renounced within six months of attaining full age.

**By registration.** The Central Government may register persons who are not illegal migrants and fall into these groups: persons of Indian origin resident in India for seven years; persons of Indian origin resident in any country outside undivided India; those married to an Indian citizen and resident for seven years; minor children of citizens; adults whose parents are registered citizens; adults who, or whose parent, were once citizens of independent India and who have lived in India for twelve months; and OCI cardholders of five years who have lived in India for twelve months. A person is of Indian origin if self or a parent was born in undivided India or in territory that became part of India after 15 August 1947. An oath of allegiance is required.

**By naturalisation.** A certificate may be granted to a non-illegal-migrant who meets the conditions below.

- Is not a citizen of a country that bars Indians from naturalisation, and undertakes to renounce any other citizenship.
- Has lived in India (or served a government in India) for twelve months immediately before applying, and for at least eleven of the fourteen years before that period.
- Is of good character, knows a language in the Eighth Schedule and intends to reside or serve in India.

The Government may waive these for distinguished service to science, philosophy, art, literature, world peace or human progress. The 2019 amendment shortens the eleven-year requirement to **five years** for Hindu, Sikh, Buddhist, Jain, Parsi and Christian migrants from Afghanistan, Bangladesh and Pakistan. Naturalised citizens take an oath to the Constitution.

**By incorporation of territory.** When foreign territory joins India, the Government decides which of its people become citizens from the notified date. The Citizenship (Pondicherry) Order of 1962 is the example.54:T495,The Citizenship (Amendment) Act, 1985 gave effect to the Assam Accord. Persons of Indian origin who came from Bangladesh before 1 January 1966 and stayed in Assam are deemed citizens from that date. Those who arrived between 1 January 1966 and 25 March 1971 and were detected as foreigners must register, and become citizens ten years after detection, though without voting rights in the interim.

The **Citizenship (Amendment) Act, 2019** applies to Hindu, Sikh, Buddhist, Jain, Parsi and Christian migrants from Afghanistan, Bangladesh or Pakistan who entered India by 31 December 2014 and were exempted from penal consequences under the Passport (Entry into India) Act, 1920 and the Foreigners Act, 1946. They may get registration or naturalisation, and are deemed citizens from the date of entry; pending illegal-migration cases abate. The provisions do not apply to Sixth Schedule tribal areas of Assam, Meghalaya, Mizoram and Tripura or areas under the Inner Line. Earlier, such migrants were illegal migrants; the Government exempted them from penal consequences in 2015 and made them eligible for long-term visas in 2016. The Act came into force on 10 January 2020.55:T42c,The 1955 Act prescribes three ways, applicable to citizens by the Constitution or by the Act.

| Mode | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Renunciation | An adult of full capacity declares renunciation; on registration the person ceases to be a citizen. Withheld during a war. A minor child also loses citizenship but may resume it within one year of turning 18 |
| Termination | Automatic when a citizen voluntarily acquires another country's citizenship; does not apply during a war |
| Deprivation | Compulsory loss by Central Government order |

Deprivation applies where citizenship was obtained by fraud; where the citizen showed disloyalty to the Constitution; where the citizen traded or communicated with the enemy in a war; where, within five years of registration or naturalisation, the citizen was jailed for two years in any country; or where the citizen has lived outside India continuously for seven years. The seven-year ground does not apply to students abroad, government or international-organisation staff, or those who register annually at an Indian consulate.56:T77f,The L. M. Singhvi High Level Committee on the Indian Diaspora (set up in September 2000, reported January 2002) urged dual citizenship for persons of Indian origin. The Citizenship (Amendment) Act, 2003 created Overseas Citizenship of India for persons of Indian origin in 16 specified countries other than Pakistan and Bangladesh; the 2005 amendment widened it to all countries except those two, provided their home laws allow dual citizenship. OCI is **not** dual citizenship, since Article 9 and the Constitution forbid it. The Citizenship (Amendment) Act, 2015 merged the PIO card scheme (begun 19 August 2002) with the OCI card scheme (from 2 December 2005), and existing PIO holders were deemed OCI cardholders from 9 January 2015.

An OCI cardholder does **not** have: equality in public employment (Article 16); eligibility for President (Article 58), Vice-President (Article 66), Supreme Court judge (Article 124) or High Court judge (Article 217); the right to vote; membership of Parliament or state legislatures; or appointment to public services except as specified. Registration may be cancelled for fraud, disaffection towards the Constitution, trading with the enemy in war, a two-year sentence within five years, violation of the Act, interests of sovereignty or security, or dissolution of the marriage on which eligibility rested; a hearing is given first. Pakistani or Bangladeshi ancestry makes a person ineligible.

| Feature | NRI | PIO | OCI cardholder |
|---|---|---|---|
| Status | Indian citizen living abroad, Indian passport | Foreign national with Indian ancestry | Registered foreign national under the 1955 Act |
| Visa | Not needed | Needed | Lifelong multiple-entry visa built in |
| Police registration | No | Yes, if stay exceeds 180 days | No |
| Path to citizenship | Already a citizen | Seven years' residence, then registration | Five years as OCI plus twelve months' residence |57:T4e9,| Category | Articles | Core content |
|---|---|---|
| Right to equality | 14–18 | Equality before law; no discrimination; equal chance in public jobs; untouchability abolished; titles abolished |
| Right to freedom | 19–22 | Six freedoms; protection on conviction; life and personal liberty; elementary education (21A); safeguards on arrest and detention |
| Right against exploitation | 23–24 | No trafficking or forced labour; no child labour in factories, mines and hazardous work |
| Right to freedom of religion | 25–28 | Conscience and religion; denominational affairs; no tax for a religion; no compulsory religious instruction |
| Cultural and educational rights | 29–30 | Language, script and culture; minority educational institutions |
| Right to constitutional remedies | 32 | Moving the Supreme Court, including the five writs |

The seventh original category, the right to property under Article 31 (with Article 19(1)(f)), was removed by the **44th Amendment Act, 1978** and survives only as a legal right in **Article 300A** of Part XII.

Only five rights are reserved for citizens: **Articles 15, 16, 19, 29 and 30**. The rest, including Articles 14, 20–22, 23–24 and 25–28, also protect foreigners other than enemy aliens.58:T624,The rights are **qualified**: the State may restrict them reasonably, and courts judge reasonableness. All bind the State and a few bind private persons; some are **negative** limits on power, others **positive** benefits. They are **justiciable**, and a person may go straight to the Supreme Court. Parliament can curtail them only by constitutional amendment, and never so as to damage the basic structure.

During a National Emergency their enforcement may be suspended, except for Articles 20 and 21; the Article 19 freedoms lapse automatically only when the emergency rests on war or external aggression, not armed rebellion. Articles 31A–31C, 33 and 34 limit them further, and the few that need a law to operate can be given effect only by Parliament (Article 35).

**Article 12** defines "State" broadly for Part III: the Union executive and Parliament, state governments and legislatures, all local authorities such as municipalities and panchayats, and "other authorities", statutory or not, such as LIC, ONGC and SAIL. The Supreme Court has added that even a private body acting as an instrument of the State falls within the term.

**Article 13** voids any law clashing with a Fundamental Right, which is the textual root of judicial review by the Supreme Court and High Courts. "Law" includes statutes, ordinances, rules, bye-laws, notifications and custom with legal force. A constitutional amendment is excluded from that definition, yet **Kesavananda Bharati (1973)** held that an amendment violating a right within the basic structure can be struck down.59:Ta3f,**Article 14** forbids the State to deny any person equality before the law or the equal protection of the laws. It covers citizens, foreigners and legal persons such as companies and registered societies. The two halves come from different traditions:

| Equality before law | Equal protection of laws |
|---|---|
| British origin | American origin |
| No special privilege for anyone; everyone subject to the ordinary law and ordinary courts; nobody above the law | Like treatment in like circumstances; the same law applied to all similarly placed persons |
| A negative idea | A positive idea |

Article 14 bars class legislation but allows **reasonable classification** based on an intelligible differentia. Equality before law belongs to A. V. Dicey's **Rule of Law** — no arbitrary power, equal subjection to ordinary law, and individual rights as the source of the constitution. India adopts the first two only, since here the Constitution is the source of rights; the Court treats the rule of law as a basic feature.

Equality has recognised exceptions: the immunities of the President and Governors under **Article 361** (for example, no criminal case or arrest process while in office); protection for true reports of legislative proceedings (Article 361A); legislators' freedom of speech and vote (Articles 105 and 194); laws under **Article 31C** for Article 39(b) and (c), where the Court said Article 14 "goes out"; and immunity for foreign rulers, diplomats and UN agencies.

**Article 15** forbids State discrimination against citizens where the **only** ground is religion, race, caste, sex or birthplace. Its second clause reaches private persons too, for access to shops, hotels and places of entertainment and the use of wells, tanks, ghats and roads open to the public. It then permits special provisions:

| Clause | What it allows | Added by |
|---|---|---|
| 15(3) | Special provisions for women and children | Original |
| 15(4) | Advancement of socially and educationally backward classes, SCs and STs | 1st Amendment, 1951 |
| 15(5) | Their admission to educational institutions, private ones included, minority institutions excepted | 93rd Amendment, 2005 |
| 15(6) | Economically weaker sections; up to 10% of seats, over and above existing quotas | 103rd Amendment, 2019 |

Under 15(5), a 2006 Act gave OBCs 27% of seats in central institutions such as IITs and IIMs; the Supreme Court upheld it in 2008 but required the **creamy layer** to be left out. The creamy-layer income limit rose from ₹1 lakh (1993) to ₹8 lakh (2017), and EWS status also needs a family income below ₹8 lakh.5a:T806,**Article 16** gives citizens an equal chance at posts under the State; to the Article 15 grounds it adds descent and residence. Its exceptions, clause by clause:

| Clause | Content | Added by |
|---|---|---|
| 16(3) | Parliament may prescribe residence for certain posts (now only in Andhra Pradesh and Telangana) | Original |
| 16(4) | Reservation for backward classes not adequately represented | Original |
| 16(4A) | Reservation in promotion for SCs and STs; consequential seniority | 77th (1995); 85th (2001) |
| 16(4B) | Unfilled reserved vacancies carried forward outside that year's 50% ceiling | 81st (2000) |
| 16(5) | Office-holders of religious institutions may be required to profess that religion | Original |
| 16(6) | Up to 10% of posts for economically weaker sections | 103rd (2019) |

The **Mandal Commission** (1979; report 1980) proposed 27% of central jobs for OBCs, adopted in 1990.
