In **Indra Sawhney (1992)** the Court upheld that 27% but rejected an extra 10% for economically backward groups, and required exclusion of the creamy layer, no reservation in promotions and a 50% ceiling save in extraordinary cases. Parliament later reversed parts of this (77th, 81st, 82nd and 85th Amendments), and the 102nd Amendment (2018) gave the National Commission for Backward Classes constitutional status under Article 338B.

**Article 17** abolishes untouchability and makes its practice punishable; the 1955 Act was renamed the Protection of Civil Rights Act in 1976. The word is undefined, and courts read it as the historical caste practice, not mere social boycott. In the **People's Union for Democratic Rights case (1982)** the Court held that it binds private persons too.

**Article 18** lets the State confer no title other than a military or academic distinction, stops citizens accepting foreign titles, and makes foreigners in State office seek the President's consent. In **Balaji Raghavan (1995)** the Court held that the Bharat Ratna and Padma awards are not titles, provided they are not used as prefixes or suffixes.5b:T78c,Article 19 protects six freedoms of **citizens only**, and only against State action. The seventh original freedom — to acquire, hold and dispose of property, 19(1)(f) — was dropped in 1978. Each freedom may be restricted only on the grounds listed in its own clause:

| Freedom | Clause | Permitted grounds of restriction |
|---|---|---|
| Speech and expression | 19(1)(a) / 19(2) | Security of the State; public order; India's sovereignty and integrity; foreign relations; decency or morality; defamation; contempt of court; incitement to an offence |
| Assembly, peaceful and unarmed | 19(1)(b) / 19(3) | Sovereignty and integrity; public order |
| Associations, unions, co-operative societies | 19(1)(c) / 19(4) | Sovereignty and integrity; public order; morality |
| Movement throughout India | 19(1)(d) / 19(5) | General public interest; protection of Scheduled Tribes |
| Residence and settlement | 19(1)(e) / 19(5) | General public interest; protection of Scheduled Tribes |
| Profession, occupation, trade or business | 19(1)(g) / 19(6) | General public interest; professional qualifications; State trading or monopoly |

The 1st Amendment (1951) added friendly relations, public order and incitement; the 16th (1963) added sovereignty and integrity; the 97th (2011) added co-operative societies.

Courts read **speech** widely — press freedom, advertisements, the right to know, silence, no pre-censorship, voters knowing candidates' antecedents, flying the flag — and it covers demonstrations but **not a right to strike**. **Association** includes the right not to join, but a union has no Fundamental Right to recognition. **Movement** inside India falls under Article 19, while going abroad falls under Article 21. Under **19(1)(g)** the State may set qualifications and even run a monopoly. **Anuradha Bhasin (2020)** extended protection to trade over the internet and ruled out indefinite internet shutdowns.5c:Tb13,**Article 20** shields any accused — citizen, foreigner or company — three ways: no **ex-post-facto** criminal law, no **double jeopardy** and no **self-incrimination**. The first reaches only criminal punishment, not retrospective tax or civil liability. Double jeopardy applies only before courts and judicial tribunals, not departmental inquiries. Self-incrimination covers oral and documentary evidence, but not thumb impressions, signatures or blood samples.

**Article 21** says no person shall lose life or personal liberty except by procedure established by law. The meaning shifted sharply:

| Gopalan (1950) | Maneka Gandhi (1978) |
|---|---|
| Narrow view | Wide view; overruled Gopalan |
| Protection only against the executive | Protection against arbitrary laws too |
| Any law-backed procedure suffices | The procedure must be fair, just and reasonable — in effect "due process" |
| Personal liberty means bodily liberty | Life means a life of dignity, not bare survival |

Later cases built a long list of rights inside Article 21, including:

| Case (year) | Right recognised |
|---|---|
| Satwant Singh Sawhney (1967) | Travel abroad |
| M. H. Hoskot (1978) | Free legal aid |
| Hussainara Khatoon (1979) | Speedy trial |
| Francis Coralie Mullin (1981) | Dignity; a prisoner's basic needs |
| Olga Tellis (1985) | Livelihood |
| Parmanand Katara (1989) | Emergency medical aid |
| K. S. Puttaswamy (2017) | Privacy |
| Common Cause (2018) | Dying with dignity (passive euthanasia) |

**Article 21A**, added by the **86th Amendment (2002)**, obliges the State to give free and compulsory education to every child aged six to fourteen; it covers elementary, not higher, education. The same amendment rewrote Article 45 to cover early childhood care up to six years and added a parental duty in Article 51A. The Court had already found a right to free education up to fourteen within Article 21 in **Unni Krishnan (1993)**, overruling the broader **Mohini Jain (1992)** view. The RTE Act, 2009 and the amendment both took effect on **1 April 2010**.

**Article 22** distinguishes punitive detention (after trial) from **preventive detention** (without trial, to stop a feared offence).

| Ordinary arrest | Preventive detention |
|---|---|
| Told the grounds of arrest | Told the grounds, though some facts may be withheld in the public interest |
| May consult a lawyer | May make a representation |
| Before a magistrate within 24 hours, travel excluded | Over three months only if an advisory board of High Court judges agrees |
| Not for enemy aliens | Citizens and aliens alike |

The 44th Amendment's cut to two months has never been brought into force. Only Parliament may make detention laws for defence, foreign affairs and India's security; states share the power on public order and essential supplies.5d:T5b4,**Article 23** bans human trafficking, *begar* (unpaid compulsory work) and similar forced labour, against private persons as well as the State; "force" includes economic compulsion such as sub-minimum wages. The State may still impose unpaid public service, such as military service, without discrimination. **Article 24** bars children under 14 from factories, mines and hazardous work, not from harmless work.

**Article 25** gives every person freedom of conscience and of religion; propagation does not include converting others. It yields to public order, morality, health and the other rights, and lets the State regulate secular activity tied to religion and open Hindu public temples to all. Carrying a kirpan counts as professing Sikhism, and "Hindus" here include Sikhs, Jains and Buddhists.

| Article | Holder | Content |
|---|---|---|
| 25 | Individuals | Conscience; profess, practise, propagate |
| 26 | Religious denominations | Run religious and charitable institutions, manage own religious affairs, own and administer property |
| 27 | Any person | No compulsion to pay a **tax** for a particular religion (a fee for services is allowed) |
| 28 | Students | No religious instruction in wholly State-funded institutions; consent needed in recognised or aided ones |

Unlike Article 25, Article 26 is not subject to the other Fundamental Rights. The Ramakrishna Mission and Ananda Marga count as denominations; the Aurobindo Society does not.5e:T7d7,**Article 32** makes the right to move the Supreme Court itself a Fundamental Right. Dr Ambedkar called it the heart and soul of the Constitution, and the Court treats it as a basic feature. Parliament may let other courts issue writs (it has not done so), and the right can be suspended only through a presidential order under **Article 359** in a National Emergency. It lies only where a Fundamental Right is at stake, and though another remedy is no bar, the Court prefers petitioners to try the High Court first.

| Point | Supreme Court (Art. 32) | High Court (Art. 226) |
|---|---|---|
| Purpose | Fundamental Rights only | Fundamental Rights and any other legal right — wider |
| Territory | Whole of India — wider | Within its territory, or where the cause of action arises there |
| Nature | Itself a Fundamental Right; the Court cannot refuse | Discretionary; the court may decline |

The writs come from English "prerogative writs". Before 1950 only the High Courts of Calcutta, Bombay and Madras could issue them.

| Writ | Literal sense | Used for | Not available |
|---|---|---|---|
| Habeas corpus | "To have the body of" | Producing a detainee and freeing him if held illegally; lies even against private persons | Lawful or court-ordered detention; contempt |
| Mandamus | "We command" | Ordering a public official or body to do a public duty | Private persons, discretionary duties, the President or Governors |
| Prohibition | "To forbid" | Stopping a lower court or tribunal before it exceeds jurisdiction | Administrative authorities |
| Certiorari | "To be certified" | Quashing a lower body's order after it is passed; now also against administrative bodies affecting rights | — |
| Quo warranto | "By what authority" | Testing a claim to a substantive public office created by law | Ministerial or private offices |

Prohibition and certiorari are both jurisdictional writs; the first acts before a final order, the second after. Any public-minded person may seek quo warranto.5f:T5cc,Compensation disputes made property the most contested right, until the 44th Amendment replaced Articles 19(1)(f) and 31 with **Article 300A**. As a legal right, property can be curtailed by ordinary law, guards only against the executive, carries no guaranteed compensation and is enforced under Article 226, not Article 32. Part III still guarantees compensation for a minority institution's property (Article 30) and for land within the ceiling under personal cultivation (Article 31A, 17th Amendment).

| Article | Protects | Key limits |
|---|---|---|
| 31A (1st Amendment) | Five kinds of law — acquiring estates, taking over management of property, merging corporations, changing directors' or shareholders' rights, ending mining leases — from Articles 14 and 19 | A state law needs the President's assent |
| 31B (1st Amendment) | Any law in the **Ninth Schedule**, against all Fundamental Rights | Laws added after **24 April 1973** can be tested against the basic structure (Waman Rao, 1980; I. R. Coelho, 2007) |
| 31C (25th Amendment, 1971) | Laws for Article 39(b) and (c) from Articles 14 and 19 | Bar on judicial review struck down in Kesavananda; extension to all Directive Principles by the 42nd Amendment struck down in **Minerva Mills (1980)** |

The Ninth Schedule began with 13 laws in 1951 and now holds 282, mostly state land-reform laws. In Coelho the Court ruled that inclusion gives no blanket immunity, because judicial review is itself a basic feature.60:T4a5,- Part III, Articles 12–35; seven categories originally, six since the 44th Amendment (1978) moved property to Article 300A.
- Citizens only: Articles 15, 16, 19, 29 and 30; all others extend to foreigners.
- Article 13 voids laws, ordinances, rules and customs that violate rights; Kesavananda (1973) lets courts test amendments against the basic structure.
- Article 15(4) 1951, 15(5) 2005, 15(6) and 16(6) EWS 2019; Indra Sawhney (1992) set the 50% ceiling and creamy-layer rule.
- Article 19: six freedoms of citizens, restricted only on the grounds in clauses (2)–(6); no right to strike.
- Article 20: no ex-post-facto criminal law, no double jeopardy, no self-incrimination; Articles 20 and 21 survive an emergency.
- Maneka Gandhi (1978) overturned Gopalan (1950); Article 21 now means fair, just and reasonable procedure and a life with dignity.
- Article 21A (86th Amendment, 2002; in force 1 April 2010): free education from six to fourteen.
- Article 32 is itself a Fundamental Right and a basic feature; Article 226 is wider in purpose but discretionary.
- Articles 31A, 31B (Ninth Schedule) and 31C are exceptions; Ninth Schedule laws after 24 April 1973 can be reviewed.61:T4e8,A directive is an instruction addressed to the State about how to legislate and govern. For Part IV, "State" carries the same meaning as in Part III (Article 36), so it covers Parliament and the Union executive, state legislatures and governments, local bodies and other public authorities.

Ambedkar likened the directives to the Instrument of Instructions that the British issued to Governors under the Government of India Act, 1935. The difference, he said, was that these instructions are now given to the legislature and the executive of a free country.

Their purpose is to lay out an economic, social and political programme that gives life to the Preamble's justice, liberty, equality and fraternity. They describe a **welfare state**, the opposite of the colonial "police state" that confined itself to order and defence, and they aim at social and economic democracy.

They cannot be enforced in court, so no government can be compelled to carry them out. Yet Article 37 calls them fundamental to governance and makes it the State's duty to apply them when making laws. Courts also use them indirectly: if a law is meant to advance a directive, the judiciary may treat it as a reasonable restriction under Article 14 or Article 19 and so save it.62:T6d2,The text itself carries no classification. Writers group the directives by ideology into socialistic, Gandhian and liberal–intellectual streams.

| Stream | Core idea | Articles and what they say |
|---|---|---|
| Socialistic | Social and economic justice, welfare state | 38 minimise inequalities; 39 livelihood, fair distribution, no concentration of wealth, equal pay, protection of workers and children; 39A free legal aid; 41 work, education and public assistance; 42 humane work conditions and maternity relief; 43 living wage; 43A workers in management; 47 nutrition, living standards and public health |
| Gandhian | Village-based reconstruction | 40 village panchayats; 43 cottage industries; 43B co-operative societies; 46 interests of SCs, STs and weaker sections; 47 prohibition of intoxicating drinks and drugs; 48 ban on slaughter of cows, calves and other milch and draught cattle |
| Liberal–intellectual | Modern, rational, internationalist outlook | 44 uniform civil code; 45 early childhood care and education up to six years; 48 modern agriculture and animal husbandry; 48A environment, forests and wildlife; 49 protection of monuments of national importance; 50 separation of judiciary from executive; 51 international peace and respect for treaties and arbitration |

Some articles straddle the streams. Article 47 appears under both the socialistic group (public health) and the Gandhian group (prohibition), and Article 48 is claimed by the Gandhian group for cattle and by the liberal group for scientific farming. A useful memory hook: **panchayats, cottage industries, co-operatives, SC/ST welfare, prohibition, cow protection** are Gandhian; **UCC, environment, monuments, judicial separation, peace** are liberal.63:T4e6,B. N. Rau, the Constitutional Adviser, suggested splitting individual rights into justiciable and non-justiciable sets, and the Drafting Committee agreed. The first set became Part III and the second Part IV.

The framers kept the directives outside the courts for practical reasons: the country lacked the money to deliver them at once, its diversity and backwardness would hinder uniform application, and a new State with many pressing tasks needed freedom to choose the order, time and manner. Their real force is political. Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar remarked that no ministry answerable to the people can lightly ignore Part IV, and Ambedkar added that a government resting on popular vote would face voters if it did.

| Basis | Fundamental Rights | Directive Principles |
|---|---|---|
| Nature | Negative: restrain the State | Positive: require the State to act |
| Enforceable in court | Yes | No |
| Goal | Political democracy | Social and economic democracy |
| Sanction | Legal | Moral and political |
| Focus | Individual | Community |
| Need for a law | Operate automatically | Need legislation |
| Effect on conflicting law | Court must strike it down | Court cannot strike it down, but may uphold a law made to give effect to a directive |64:T671,| Stage | What happened |
|---|---|
| Champakam Dorairajan (1951) | Court held that Fundamental Rights prevail over directives; the directives must run as subsidiary. Parliament could, however, amend Part III. The First (1951), Fourth (1955) and Seventeenth (1964) Amendments followed to push directives |
| Golaknath (1967) | Court held Parliament cannot abridge Fundamental Rights at all, so they could not be amended to carry out directives |
| 24th and 25th Amendments (1971) | The 24th confirmed Parliament's power to amend or take away Fundamental Rights. The 25th inserted **Article 31C**: laws implementing Article 39(b) and (c) could not be voided for violating Articles 14, 19 or 31, and a law carrying a declaration to that effect could not be questioned in court |
| Kesavananda Bharati (1973) | Court struck down the bar on judicial review in Article 31C (judicial review is basic structure) but upheld the protection against challenge under Articles 14, 19 and 31 |
| 42nd Amendment (1976) | Extended Article 31C protection to laws giving effect to any directive, giving the directives primacy over Articles 14, 19 and 31 |
| Minerva Mills (1980) | Court struck down that extension. Directives again became subordinate, except that Articles 14 and 19 yield to Article 39(b) and (c). The Court said the two parts are like two wheels of a chariot and their balance is part of the basic structure |

Article 31, the right to property, was dropped by the 44th Amendment (1978). The settled position is that Fundamental Rights are supreme, but Parliament may still amend them to serve directives provided the basic structure remains intact.65:T7b0,Governments have acted on the directives through laws and programmes. Sample links worth remembering:

| Directive | Measures |
|---|---|
| Social order, reduced inequality | Planning Commission (1950), replaced in 2015 by NITI Aayog; land reform covering abolition of intermediaries, tenancy reform, land ceilings and surplus distribution |
| Workers and women | Minimum Wages Act 1948, Factories Act 1948, Industrial Disputes Act 1947, Maternity Benefit Act 1961, Equal Remuneration Act 1976, Bonded Labour System Abolition Act 1976; child labour law renamed in 2016 to cover adolescents |
| Common good of resources | Nationalisation of life insurance (1956), fourteen banks (1969), general insurance (1971); Privy Purses abolished (1971) |
| Free legal aid (39A) | Legal Services Authorities Act 1987 and lok adalats, whose awards are final and not appealable |
| Panchayats and cottage industries | Three-tier panchayati raj; 73rd Amendment (1992); Khadi and Village Industries Commission and similar boards |
| Weaker sections | Reservations; Protection of Civil Rights Act (renamed 1976); SC/ST Atrocities Act 1989; National Commission for SCs and STs (65th Amendment 1990), split into two by the 89th (2003); constitutional status for the Backward Classes Commission by the 102nd (2018); 10% EWS quota through the 103rd (2019) |
| Environment | Wildlife (Protection) Act 1972, Forest (Conservation) Act 1980, Water and Air Acts with pollution control boards, National Forest Policy 1988 |
| Judicial separation (50) | Criminal Procedure Code 1973 moved judicial functions from collectors and tehsildars to magistrates under the High Court |
| Monuments (49) | Ancient and Historical Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act 1951 |
| Peace (51) | Non-alignment and Panchsheel |

Despite this record, full implementation has been held back by scarce funds, difficult socio-economic conditions, population pressure and strained Centre–state relations.66:T479,- Part IV covers Articles 36–51; Article 36 defines State, Article 37 gives non-enforceability plus a duty to apply, and the actual directives are Articles 38–51.
- Source of the idea: Irish Constitution of 1937; Ambedkar compared them to the 1935 Act's Instrument of Instructions.
- Three streams: socialistic, Gandhian, liberal–intellectual; the Constitution itself does not classify them.
- 42nd Amendment added four (child development in 39, 39A, 43A, 48A); 44th added the inequalities clause in 38; 86th recast Article 45; 97th added 43B.
- Directives aim at social and economic democracy and a welfare state; Fundamental Rights aim at political democracy.
- Champakam: FR over DP. Golaknath: FR cannot be amended. Kesavananda: judicial-review bar in Article 31C void.
- Minerva Mills: extension of Article 31C to all directives struck down; FR–DP harmony is basic structure.
- Present position: FR supreme, but they may be amended for directives without hurting basic structure.
- Directives outside Part IV: Articles 335, 350A and 351.
- Legal Services Authorities Act 1987 implements Article 39A; CrPC 1973 implements Article 50.67:T731,Constitutions of liberal democracies such as the USA, Canada, France, Germany and Australia carry no list of citizen duties; Japan is perhaps the only democratic exception. The Indian idea is traced to the Constitution of the former USSR, where the enjoyment of rights was tied to the performance of obligations. Socialist systems gave duties equal weight with rights.

In 1976 the Congress party appointed the **Sardar Swaran Singh Committee**, prompted by experience during the internal Emergency of 1975–77. It advised a separate chapter on duties so that citizens would realise that rights carry responsibilities. The government accepted this and passed the **42nd Amendment Act (1976)**, which created **Part IVA** with a single article, **Article 51A**. The ruling party described the earlier omission as a historical mistake that was now being corrected.

The committee had proposed eight duties; the amendment inserted ten. The government also left out three of its suggestions.

| Committee proposal | Outcome |
|---|---|
| Separate chapter on duties | Accepted (Part IVA) |
| Parliament may prescribe penalty for failing to observe a duty | Not accepted |
| Such penal laws to be shielded from challenge under Fundamental Rights | Not accepted |
| Paying taxes to be a duty | Not accepted |

Law Minister H. R. Gokhale justified the move by pointing to attitudes before the Emergency, saying a chapter on duties would have a sobering effect on agitators. Indira Gandhi argued that the aim was a democratic balance in which people are as conscious of duties as of rights. The Opposition resisted, yet the Janata government that followed, while undoing many 42nd Amendment changes through the 43rd (1977) and 44th (1978) Amendments, left Part IVA in place. That is read as an eventual consensus, reinforced by the new duty of 2002.68:T4b0,Under Article 51A, every citizen of India is expected to:

| No. | Duty in brief |
|---|---|
| 1 | Follow the Constitution and respect its ideals and institutions, the National Flag and the National Anthem |
| 2 | Cherish the noble ideals of the national struggle for freedom |
| 3 | Uphold and protect the sovereignty, unity and integrity of India |
| 4 | Defend the country and render national service when called upon |
| 5 | Promote harmony and common brotherhood across religious, linguistic and regional divides, and give up practices demeaning to women |
| 6 | Value and preserve the country's composite cultural heritage |
| 7 | Protect and improve the natural environment, with compassion for living creatures |
| 8 | Develop scientific temper, humanism and the spirit of inquiry and reform |
| 9 | Safeguard public property and abjure violence |
| 10 | Strive for excellence in individual and collective activity |
| 11 | Provide opportunities for education to a child or ward between six and fourteen years (added by the 86th Amendment, 2002) |

Items 1 to 10 came in 1976; item 11 came in 2002. The 2002 amendment also made elementary education a Fundamental Right and rewrote Article 45.69:T504,The steps run in a fixed order.

1. **Initiation.** Only by a bill in either House of Parliament; state legislatures cannot start the process. A minister or a private member can move it, and the President's prior permission is not needed.
2. **Voting.** Each House passes the bill separately by a **special majority**: a majority of the total membership of the House plus two-thirds of members present and voting.
3. **No joint sitting.** If the Houses disagree, there is no provision for a joint sitting, unlike for ordinary bills.
4. **State ratification.** If the bill touches federal provisions, legislatures of at least half the states must ratify it by a simple majority of members present and voting.
5. **Assent.** The bill goes to the President, who must give assent. The 24th Amendment (1971) removed any discretion: the President can neither withhold assent nor send the bill back.
6. **Result.** After assent the bill becomes a constitutional amendment Act and the Constitution stands changed as the Act provides.

The phrase "total membership" means the full strength of the House, counting vacancies and absentees. The Lok Sabha rules insist on the special majority at all effective stages of the bill, even though the Article, on its face, applies it at the final vote.6a:T640,Article 368 itself contains two methods. A third works outside it: some articles allow change by ordinary law, and such changes are not treated as amendments for the purpose of Article 368.

| Route | Majority needed | Typical subjects |
|---|---|---|
| Simple majority of Parliament | Majority of members present and voting in each House, as for an ordinary bill | Admission, formation and boundary or name change of states; legislative councils; emoluments of President, Governors, Speakers and judges (Second Schedule); quorum, salaries, rules of procedure and privileges in Parliament; English in Parliament; official language; number of Supreme Court judges and extra jurisdiction; citizenship; elections and delimitation; Union territories; Fifth and Sixth Schedules |
| Special majority of Parliament | Majority of total membership plus two-thirds of those present and voting, in each House | Fundamental Rights, Directive Principles, and every provision not covered by the other two routes |
| Special majority plus half of the states | Special majority in Parliament and simple majority in legislatures of half the states | Election of the President; extent of Union and state executive power; Supreme Court and High Courts; distribution of legislative powers; Seventh Schedule lists; representation of states in Parliament; Article 368 itself; GST Council (added by the 101st Amendment, 2016) |

On state ratification, the moment half of the states approve, the process is complete. Silence from the others does not matter, and the Constitution sets no time limit for the states to respond.6b:T463,Critics raise several points.

- **No special body.** There is no constitutional convention or assembly, as in the USA, to carry out amendments; the power rests with Parliament and, in a few cases, state legislatures.
- **Initiation only by Parliament.** The sole case in which a state legislature can start something is a resolution asking Parliament to create or abolish its legislative council, and Parliament may ignore it. In the USA a convention can be called on petition of two-thirds of the state legislatures.
- **Little role for states.** Most of the Constitution can be changed by Parliament alone, and where states are needed only half must agree, compared with three-fourths in the USA.
- **Gaps on ratification.** No time limit, and no rule on whether a state may withdraw consent once given.
- **No deadlock mechanism.** There is no joint sitting for amendment bills even though it exists for ordinary bills.
- **Looks like ordinary lawmaking.** Apart from the special majority, the process is the same as for an ordinary bill.
- **Sketchy rules.** The thin text leaves room for disputes to reach the courts.6c:T45d,The main legal question has been whether the amending power can reach Fundamental Rights. The position developed in stages:

| Stage | Position |
|---|---|
| Golaknath (1967) | Court held that Parliament cannot take away or abridge Fundamental Rights through Article 368 |
| 24th Amendment (1971) | Declared Parliament's power to amend any provision, including Fundamental Rights; made presidential assent compulsory |
| Kesavananda Bharati (1973) | Parliament may amend any part, including Fundamental Rights, but not the basic structure |
| 42nd Amendment (1976) | Added clauses to Article 368 declaring that amending power had no limits and that no court could question an amendment |
| Minerva Mills (1980) | Court struck down those clauses, holding that limited amending power and judicial review belong to the basic structure |

The result is that Parliament's amending power is wide but not unlimited. An amendment is valid only if it follows the Article 368 procedure and leaves the basic structure intact. The topic on the basic structure of the Constitution develops the doctrine and its features in detail.6d:T405,The core issue was whether a constitutional amendment counts as a "law" under Article 13, which voids laws that violate Fundamental Rights.

| Case | Year | Held |
|---|---|---|
| Shankari Prasad | 1951 | Challenge to the First Amendment, which curtailed the right to property. Court held that the power under Article 368 includes power over Fundamental Rights, and "law" in Article 13 means ordinary law only |
| Sajjan Singh | 1964 | Reaffirmed Shankari Prasad: an amendment under Article 368 is not "law" under Article 13 |
| Golak Nath | 1967 | Challenge to the Seventeenth Amendment, which placed certain state Acts in the Ninth Schedule. Court reversed course: Fundamental Rights hold a "transcendental and immutable" place; an amendment is a law under Article 13 and is void if it violates them |

Parliament answered Golak Nath with the **24th Amendment (1971)**, which changed Articles 13 and 368 to say that Parliament can abridge or remove Fundamental Rights and that such an amendment is not a "law" under Article 13.6e:T6e4,In this landmark ruling the Supreme Court overturned Golak Nath and upheld the 24th Amendment. Parliament, it said, may abridge or remove Fundamental Rights. At the same time the Court laid down a new doctrine: the constituent power under Article 368 cannot alter the **basic structure** of the Constitution. So a Fundamental Right that forms part of the basic structure cannot be taken away. The case is also called the Fundamental Rights Case.

The doctrine was applied again in **Indira Nehru Gandhi v. Raj Narain (1975)**, the Election Case. The Court struck down a clause of the 39th Amendment (1975) that placed election disputes involving the Prime Minister and the Speaker beyond the reach of all courts, because it damaged the basic structure.

Parliament reacted with the **42nd Amendment (1976)**, which amended Article 368 to declare that its power was without limit and that no amendment could be questioned in any court, including on the ground of violating Fundamental Rights.

In **Minerva Mills (1980)** the Court invalidated that change because it shut out judicial review, a basic feature. Its reasoning was that a limited amending power is itself a basic feature, so Parliament cannot use that limited power to turn it into an absolute one; the holder of a limited power cannot enlarge it by using it.

In **Waman Rao (1980)** the Court confirmed that the doctrine applies to amendments made after 24 April 1973, the date of the Kesavananda judgment.

| Step | Court | Parliament's response |
|---|---|---|
| Golak Nath (1967) | Rights beyond amendment | 24th Amendment (1971) |
| Kesavananda Bharati (1973) | Basic structure limit | 42nd Amendment (1976) |
| Minerva Mills (1980) | 42nd Amendment clauses void | None needed; position settled |6f:T5d2,The present rule is that Parliament may amend any part of the Constitution, including Fundamental Rights, without affecting the basic structure. The Court has not issued a closed list, and elements have emerged case by case. The commonly cited elements are:

| Theme | Elements |
|---|---|
| Foundation | Supremacy of the Constitution; sovereign, democratic and republican polity; secular character; unity and integrity of the nation |
| Distribution of power | Separation of powers; federal character; parliamentary system |
| Rights and justice | Welfare state (socio-economic justice); freedom and dignity of the individual; principle of equality; harmony between Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles; principles underlying Fundamental Rights |
| Judiciary and law | Judicial review; independence of the judiciary; rule of law; effective access to justice; powers of the Supreme Court under Articles 32, 136, 141 and 142; powers of High Courts under Articles 226 and 227 |
| Democracy | Free and fair elections; limited amending power of Parliament |

Two points deserve emphasis. First, the doctrine tests an amendment, not an ordinary law; an ordinary Act is tested against the Constitution itself. Second, the Court treats the doctrine as a shield for the identity of the Constitution: Parliament may repair or update the text, but it may not replace the framework on which the text rests, for instance by abolishing judicial review or turning the republic into something else.70:T7f1,| Case (year) | Element declared |
|---|---|
| Kesavananda Bharati (1973) | Supremacy of the Constitution; separation of powers; republican and democratic government; secularism; federalism; sovereignty and unity of India; dignity of the individual; welfare state mandate; parliamentary system |
| Indira Nehru Gandhi (1975) | Sovereign democratic republic; equality of status and opportunity; secularism and freedom of conscience; rule of law; judicial review; free and fair elections |
| Minerva Mills (1980) | Limited amending power; judicial review; balance between Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles |
| Central Coal Fields (1980) | Effective access to justice |
| Bhim Singhji (1980) | Welfare state, that is socio-economic justice |
| S. P. Sampath Kumar (1986) and P. Sambamurthy (1986) | Rule of law; judicial review |
| Delhi Judicial Service Association (1991) | Supreme Court powers under Articles 32, 136, 141, 142 |
| Indra Sawhney (1992), the Mandal Case | Rule of law |
| Kumar Padma Prasad (1992) | Independence of the judiciary |
| Kihoto Hollohan (1992), the Defection Case | Free and fair elections; sovereign, democratic and republican structure |
| Raghunath Rao (1993) | Equality; unity and integrity of India |
| S. R. Bommai (1994) | Federalism; secularism; democracy; unity and integrity; social justice; judicial review |
| L. Chandra Kumar (1997) | High Court powers under Articles 226 and 227 |
| Indra Sawhney II (1999) | Principle of equality |
| All India Judges' Association (2001) | Independent judicial system |
| Kuldip Nayar (2006) | Democracy; free and fair elections |
| M. Nagaraj (2006) | Principle of equality |
| I. R. Coelho (2007), the Ninth Schedule Case | Rule of law; separation of powers; essence of Fundamental Rights; judicial review; equality |
| Ram Jethmalani (2011) | Supreme Court powers under Article 32 |
| Namit Sharma (2012) | Freedom and dignity of the individual |
| Madras Bar Association (2014) | Judicial review; High Court powers under Articles 226 and 227 |71:T5e1,The President may proclaim a National Emergency under Article 352 when the security of India, or of any part of it, is threatened by war, external aggression or armed rebellion. The threat need not have materialised; an imminent danger is enough if the President is satisfied it exists. An emergency on the ground of war or external aggression is called an **External Emergency**; one based on armed rebellion is an **Internal Emergency**.

Several amendments shaped this power:

| Amendment | Change |
|---|---|
| 38th (1975) | President may issue separate proclamations on different grounds even while one is already in force; proclamation made immune from judicial review |
| 42nd (1976) | Operation of an emergency may be confined to a specified part of India |
| 44th (1978) | "Armed rebellion" replaced the vague phrase "internal disturbance"; proclamation only on the **written advice of the cabinet**; the bar on judicial review removed |

The cabinet rule answers a specific episode. In 1975 Prime Minister Indira Gandhi advised the President to proclaim emergency without consulting her colleagues, who learnt of it only after the event. After 1978 the cabinet — the Prime Minister and the ministers of cabinet rank — must recommend in writing, so a Prime Minister cannot act alone. In **Minerva Mills (1980)** the Supreme Court confirmed that a proclamation can be questioned in court if it is mala fide, or rests on facts that are wholly extraneous or irrelevant, or is absurd or perverse.72:T5af,Both Houses must approve the proclamation within **one month**; the window was two months until the 44th Amendment shortened it. If the Lok Sabha is dissolved when the proclamation is issued, or is dissolved within that month without having approved it, the proclamation survives until 30 days after the reconstituted Lok Sabha first sits, provided the Rajya Sabha has approved it meanwhile.

Once approved, the emergency lasts **six months** and can be continued without any upper limit, provided Parliament approves it again every six months. Before 1978 a single approval let it run as long as the government wished. Each approving resolution needs a **special majority** in each House: a majority of the total membership and two-thirds of those present and voting. That too is a 1978 addition; earlier a simple majority sufficed.

The President may revoke the proclamation at any time by a fresh proclamation, which needs no parliamentary approval. Since 1978 the President **must** revoke it if the Lok Sabha passes a resolution disapproving its continuance. If one-tenth of the Lok Sabha's total membership gives written notice to the Speaker (or to the President when the House is not in session), a special sitting must be held within **14 days** to consider such a resolution.

| | Resolution approving continuance | Resolution disapproving continuance |
|---|---|---|
| Passed by | Both Houses | Lok Sabha alone |
| Majority | Special | Simple |73:T573,The states keep functioning during a National Emergency, but the Centre gains overriding powers in three spheres.

- **Executive:** in normal times the Union can direct a state only on specified matters; now it can direct a state on any matter. State governments are controlled, not suspended.
- **Legislative:** Parliament may legislate on any State List subject. State legislatures are not suspended, but their laws yield to Parliament's. Such Union laws lapse six months after the emergency ends. The President may also issue ordinances on state subjects when Parliament is not sitting, and Parliament may confer powers and duties on Union officers for matters outside the Union List. The 42nd Amendment extended these executive and legislative effects even to states where the emergency is not in operation.
- **Financial:** the President may alter the constitutional sharing of revenues, reducing or cancelling transfers to the states. The change lasts until the end of the financial year in which the emergency ends, and each such order must be laid before both Houses.

Parliament may also extend the five-year term of the Lok Sabha by law, one year at a time, but not beyond six months after the emergency ceases. The term of a state legislative assembly can be stretched the same way. The **Fifth Lok Sabha (1971–77)** is the example: its life was extended twice, by a year each time.74:T699,**Article 358** frees the State from the limits of Article 19 the moment a proclamation is made; no separate order is needed. Laws and executive acts that curtail the six freedoms cannot be challenged on that ground while the emergency lasts. When it ends, Article 19 revives and inconsistent laws stop operating, yet nothing done in the meantime can be questioned afterwards.

**Article 359** works differently. It lets the President, by order, suspend the right to move a court for enforcing the Fundamental Rights named in the order. The rights remain in existence; only the remedy is put on hold. The order may cover the whole emergency or a shorter span, the whole country or part of it, and it must be placed before each House.

The 44th Amendment narrowed both. Article 358 now applies only to an emergency declared on the ground of war or external aggression, not armed rebellion. Under Article 359 the enforcement of **Articles 20 and 21** can never be suspended. And under both, only laws connected with the emergency, and executive action taken under such laws, enjoy immunity.

| Point | Article 358 | Article 359 |
|---|---|---|
| Rights affected | Article 19 only | Rights named in the Presidential Order |
| How it operates | Automatic on proclamation | Needs a Presidential Order |
| Kind of emergency | External only | External and internal |
| Period | Whole duration of the emergency | Whole duration or shorter, as the order says |
| Area | Entire country | Entire country or a part |
| Limit | Article 19 suspended entirely | Articles 20 and 21 cannot be touched |

Their common feature: only emergency-related laws, and action under them, are shielded from challenge.75:T96c,President's Rule under Article 356 can rest on two grounds. Under Article 356 itself, the President may act on being satisfied that a state's government cannot be carried on according to the Constitution — on the Governor's report or even without one. Under **Article 365**, a state's failure to comply with a Union direction allows the President to treat that as such a situation.

Both Houses must approve within **two months**, with the same Lok Sabha-dissolution rule as above. An approved proclamation lasts six months and may be extended, six months at a time, up to **three years**. Each resolution needs only a **simple majority** of members present and voting. Since the 44th Amendment, an extension beyond one year is allowed only if a National Emergency is in force in all of India or in all or part of the state, and the **Election Commission** certifies that assembly elections cannot be held. The President may revoke it at any time without parliamentary approval.

While it lasts, the President dismisses the council of ministers, takes over the state's executive functions and declares that Parliament will exercise the legislature's powers. The Governor runs the state on the President's behalf, aided by the chief secretary or advisers. The assembly is suspended or dissolved; Parliament passes the state's bills and budget, and may delegate law-making to the President, whose laws — **President's Acts** — are usually made after consulting the state's MPs. With the Lok Sabha not in session, the President may authorise spending from the state's Consolidated Fund. Such laws outlast the proclamation until the state legislature changes them. The **High Court** is untouched: its status and powers cannot be taken over or suspended.

| Point | National Emergency | President's Rule |
|---|---|---|
| Ground | Security threatened by war, aggression or armed rebellion | Constitutional breakdown, possibly unrelated to security |
| State government | Continues, Centre gets concurrent power | Executive dismissed, legislature suspended or dissolved |
| Delegation of state law-making | Not allowed | Allowed (President's Acts) |
| Maximum period | None | Three years |
| States affected | Relations with all states change | Only the state concerned |
| Majority for approval | Special | Simple |
| Fundamental Rights | Affected | Not affected |
| Lok Sabha can force revocation | Yes | No |76:T9d7,The first imposition was in **Punjab in 1951**, and by now the provision has been invoked more than 125 times, many of them for political ends. In 1977 the Janata government of Morarji Desai brought nine Congress-ruled states under it, arguing their assemblies no longer reflected the voters; the Congress returned the favour in nine states in 1980. In 1992 three BJP-ruled states — Madhya Pradesh, Himachal Pradesh and Rajasthan — were taken over for not enforcing the Centre's ban on religious organisations. Ambedkar had hoped the article would stay a "dead letter"; H. V. Kamath later quipped that Ambedkar was gone but the articles were very much alive.

The 38th Amendment had made the President's satisfaction under Article 356 final; the 44th removed that bar. In **S. R. Bommai v. Union of India (1994)** the Supreme Court upheld the 1992 action on the ground that secularism is a basic feature, but rejected the impositions in Nagaland (1988), Karnataka (1989) and Meghalaya (1991). Its main rules:

- The proclamation is open to judicial review; satisfaction must rest on relevant material, and the Centre carries the burden of showing it. Courts examine relevance, not adequacy.
- If the proclamation falls, the court can restore the dismissed ministry and revive the assembly.
- The assembly may only be suspended until Parliament approves; dissolution must wait for that approval.
- Loss of majority is to be tested on the floor of the House.
- A new party at the Centre cannot dismiss state ministries simply because they belong to rivals.
- Article 356 is an exceptional power for rare use; an anti-secular state government can, however, be acted against.

Drawing on the **Sarkaria Commission** report (1988), the Court also listed proper and improper uses.

| Proper use | Improper use |
|---|---|
| Hung assembly after elections | Governor recommends it without exploring an alternative ministry |
| Majority party will not form a ministry and no coalition is possible | Governor judges support himself instead of a floor test |
| Ministry resigns after defeat and nobody else can form one | Ruling party loses badly in Lok Sabha polls (as in 1977, 1980) |
| State disregards a constitutional direction of the Centre | Internal disturbance short of subversion or breakdown |
| Internal subversion or a fomented violent revolt | Maladministration, corruption charges or financial strain |
| Physical breakdown endangering security | No prior warning given, or used to settle intra-party disputes |77:T40d,Article 360 lets the President proclaim a Financial Emergency when the financial stability or credit of India, or of any part of its territory, is threatened. The 38th Amendment's bar on judicial review was again deleted in 1978. Approval by both Houses is needed within **two months**, by simple majority. Once approved, it continues until revoked — no maximum period and no repeated approval.

During it, the Union may direct any state to follow canons of financial propriety and other directions, including cuts in salaries and allowances of state employees and reserving money and financial bills for the President after the legislature passes them. The President may order salary cuts for Union employees and for **judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts**. H. N. Kunzru warned that this threatened state financial autonomy; Ambedkar explained it was modelled on the American **National Recovery Act of 1933**, passed during the Great Depression. No Financial Emergency has ever been declared, not even during the 1991 crisis.78:T42c,Critics feared that the provisions would wreck federalism, concentrate power in the Union executive, make the President a dictator, nullify state finances and empty Fundamental Rights of meaning. H. V. Kamath foresaw the foundation of a totalitarian, police state; K. T. Shah called the chapter one of reaction and retrogression; T. T. Krishnamachari spoke of a constitutional dictatorship. Defenders answered back: Alladi Krishnaswami Ayyar saw them as the life-breath of the Constitution, and Mahabir Tyagi as a safety valve. Ambedkar himself admitted they could be abused for political purposes.

| Article | Subject |
|---|---|
| 352 | Proclamation of Emergency |
| 353 | Effect of the proclamation |
| 354 | Distribution of revenues during an emergency |
| 355 | Union's duty to protect states |
| 356 | Failure of constitutional machinery in states |
| 357 | Legislative powers under an Article 356 proclamation |
| 358 | Suspension of Article 19 |
| 359 | Suspension of enforcement of Part III rights |
| 359A | Punjab (repealed) |
| 360 | Financial Emergency |79:T419,- Part XVIII, Articles 352–360: National Emergency (352), President's Rule (356), Financial Emergency (360).
- Grounds of 352: war, external aggression, armed rebellion ("internal disturbance" replaced in 1978).
- 352 needs written cabinet advice; approval within one month by special majority; renewal every six months, no ceiling.
- Lok Sabha alone can force revocation of 352 by simple majority; one-tenth members can demand a sitting within 14 days.
- Article 358: Article 19 suspended automatically, external emergency only; Article 359: Presidential Order, never Articles 20 and 21.
- National Emergencies: 1962, 1971, 1975; Shah Commission led to the 44th Amendment.
- 356: approval within two months by simple majority; maximum three years; beyond one year needs National Emergency plus EC certificate.
- First President's Rule: Punjab, 1951; Bommai (1994) made it reviewable and insisted on floor tests.
- 360: approval within two months, runs indefinitely; never declared.
- High Court powers can never be assumed under President's Rule.7a:T5a2,The people do not elect the President directly. An **electoral college** does, made up of:

| Included | Excluded |
|---|---|
| Elected members of the Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha | Nominated members of either House |
| Elected members of state legislative assemblies | Nominated members of assemblies |
| Elected members of the assemblies of Delhi and Puducherry (added by the 70th Amendment, 1992, effective 1995) | All members, elected or nominated, of state legislative councils; nominated members of the Puducherry assembly |

Delhi's assembly has no nominated members. The 104th Amendment of 2019 ended nomination of Anglo-Indians to the Lok Sabha and assemblies from 25 January 2020. Members of a dissolved assembly lose their vote even if the fresh election has not yet been held.

The Constitution demands **uniformity** among states and **parity** between the states taken together and the Union. Each MLA's vote is therefore weighted: divide the state's population by its number of elected MLAs, then divide by 1,000. Under the 84th Amendment, "population" means the 1971 census figure until data from the first census after 2026 is published. Each MP's vote equals the total value of all MLA votes divided by the number of elected MPs, so the two halves of the college carry nearly equal weight. In the **2022** election, for instance, an MP's vote was worth 700, and the whole college of 4,809 electors carried a value of 10,86,431.7b:T6e3,The election uses **proportional representation by single transferable vote**, with a secret ballot. Each elector gets one ballot and may rank as many candidates as there are in the contest. To win, a candidate needs a quota: total valid votes divided by two (one seat plus one), plus one — in effect an absolute majority. First preferences are counted first; if nobody reaches the quota, the lowest candidate is eliminated and that candidate's ballots pass to the next preferences, round after round, until someone does. Critics in the Assembly noted that "proportional representation" is a misnomer for a single vacancy — it is really a preferential or alternative vote — and that each voter in fact expresses several preferences rather than one vote.

The **Supreme Court** decides all disputes about the election, and its word is final. An election cannot be challenged merely because the college had vacancies. If an election is set aside, acts done by the President before the judgment remain valid.

Why indirect? The framers gave three answers. A nominal head with no real power did not need a direct popular mandate, which would sit oddly with parliamentary government. A nationwide poll for a symbolic office would be costly and exhausting. And a college of MPs alone was rejected because a single dominant party in Parliament would always pick its own nominee; including state legislators makes the President a representative of both the Union and the states.

Some election facts worth knowing: **Dr Rajendra Prasad** won in 1952 and 1957 and is the only person to hold two terms; **N. Sanjeeva Reddy** was elected unopposed in 1977; the 1969 contest between **V. V. Giri** and Sanjeeva Reddy was the closest; and **Droupadi Murmu** won in 2022.7c:T66b,A candidate must be an Indian citizen, at least **35 years** old, qualified to be elected to the Lok Sabha, and must not hold an office of profit under the Union, a state, a local authority or any other public authority. The sitting President or Vice-President, a Governor, and a Union or state minister are not treated as holding offices of profit for this purpose.

The nomination needs **50 proposers and 50 seconders** from the electoral college, and a deposit of **₹15,000** in the Reserve Bank of India, forfeited if the candidate fails to win one-sixth of the votes polled. Before 1997 the figures were ten each and ₹2,500; they were raised to deter frivolous candidates.

The **Chief Justice of India** (or, failing him, the senior-most available Supreme Court judge) administers the oath. The President swears to execute the office faithfully, to preserve, protect and defend the Constitution and the law, and to serve the well-being of the people. Anyone acting as President takes a similar oath.

Conditions of office: the President cannot be a member of Parliament or a state legislature — a member who is elected vacates that seat on assuming office — and cannot hold any other office of profit. Rashtrapati Bhavan is provided rent-free; emoluments are fixed by Parliament and cannot be cut during the term. Parliament raised the monthly salary from ₹1.5 lakh to ₹5 lakh in 2018. The President is immune from legal liability for official acts and, during the term, from criminal proceedings even for personal acts, and cannot be arrested. Civil proceedings over personal acts are possible after **two months' notice**.7d:T7e0,The term is **five years** from the day of assuming office. The President may resign by writing to the **Vice-President**, stays on beyond five years until the successor takes charge, and may be re-elected any number of times (the American President is limited to two terms).

Removal is by **impeachment** for "violation of the Constitution", a phrase the Constitution leaves undefined. The steps:

| Step | Requirement |
|---|---|
| Initiation | Either House; charges signed by one-fourth of its members |
| Notice | 14 days to the President |
| First House | Resolution passed by two-thirds of its **total membership** |
| Second House | Investigates the charges; the President may appear or be represented |
| Removal | Second House also passes by two-thirds of total membership; removal from that date |

Impeachment is quasi-judicial. Note the asymmetry: **nominated MPs take part** in impeachment though not in the election, while **elected MLAs** vote in the election but play no part in impeachment. No President has ever been impeached.

A vacancy arises on expiry of the term, resignation, removal, death, or otherwise — for example on disqualification or a voided election. If the term is ending, the election must be completed before it ends; any delay simply keeps the outgoing President in office, so the Vice-President does not step in. For any other vacancy the election must be held within **six months**, and the new President gets a full five-year term. Meanwhile the **Vice-President** acts as President, and also discharges the functions when the President is absent or ill. If that office too is vacant, the **Chief Justice of India**, or failing that the senior-most available judge, acts. Two Presidents — **Zakir Husain** and **Fakhruddin Ali Ahmed** — died in office. After Husain's death in 1969, Vice-President V. V. Giri acted, then resigned to contest, and Chief Justice **M. Hidayatullah** officiated for about a month. Whoever acts enjoys the President's powers and immunities.7e:T515,| Sphere | Main powers |
|---|---|
| Executive | All Union executive action in the President's name; rules for authenticating orders and for transaction of business; appoints the Prime Minister and ministers (who serve at pleasure), Attorney General, CAG, Election Commissioners, UPSC members, Governors, Finance Commission; may seek information from the PM and ask that a minister's decision go to the whole council; appoints a backward classes commission and an inter-state council; administers UTs; declares scheduled areas |
| Financial | Prior recommendation for money bills; causes the Budget to be laid; no demand for grants without recommendation; advances from the Contingency Fund of India; constitutes a Finance Commission every five years |
| Judicial | Appoints the Chief Justice and judges of the Supreme Court and High Courts; may seek the Supreme Court's advice on law or fact (Article 143), which is not binding; pardoning power |
| Diplomatic | Treaties concluded in the President's name, subject to Parliament's approval; sends and receives envoys |
| Military | Supreme commander of the defence forces; appoints service chiefs; declares war and concludes peace subject to Parliament |
| Emergency | National Emergency (352), President's Rule (356, 365), Financial Emergency (360) |7f:T5e2,The President is a constituent part of Parliament. The office summons and prorogues the Houses, dissolves the Lok Sabha and calls a **joint sitting**, which the Speaker chairs. The President addresses Parliament at the first session after a general election and at the first session each year, and may send messages to either House. If both presiding officers of a House are absent, the President may appoint a member to preside. Twelve members of the Rajya Sabha are nominated for distinction in literature, science, art and social service; two Anglo-Indian nominations to the Lok Sabha existed until the 104th Amendment ended them. Questions of disqualification of MPs are decided by the President **after consulting the Election Commission**.

Prior recommendation is required for several kinds of bills — those drawing on the Consolidated Fund, altering state boundaries or creating states, money bills, taxes in which states are interested, bills changing the meaning of "agricultural income", bills affecting the principles of distributing money to states, and surcharges for Union purposes. A state bill restricting freedom of trade also needs the President's prior sanction. The President lays before Parliament the reports of the CAG, UPSC and Finance Commission, issues ordinances during recess, and makes regulations for the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, Lakshadweep, Dadra and Nagar Haveli and Daman and Diu, and Ladakh — and for Puducherry only while its assembly is suspended or dissolved.80:T814,Under **Article 111** the President, faced with a bill passed by Parliament, may assent, withhold assent, or return it (unless it is a money bill) for reconsideration; if Parliament passes it again, with or without changes, assent must follow. The aim is to check hasty or unconstitutional legislation.

| Type of veto | Meaning | Indian President? |
|---|---|---|
| Absolute | Withholding assent; the bill dies | Yes |
| Qualified | Overridable only by a higher majority | No — the US President has it |
| Suspensive | Overridable by re-passing with an ordinary majority | Yes, except for money bills |
| Pocket | Taking no action, with no time limit | Yes |

The **absolute veto** is used mainly for private members' bills, or for government bills when a cabinet falls before assent and its successor advises against them. Dr Rajendra Prasad withheld assent to the **PEPSU Appropriation Bill** in 1954, and R. Venkataraman to a 1991 bill on MPs' salaries and pensions passed without his prior recommendation. The **pocket veto** exists because the Constitution sets no deadline — unlike the ten days allowed in the USA. Zail Singh used it in 1986 on the **Indian Post Office (Amendment) Bill**, criticised as curbing press freedom; Venkataraman returned it in 1989 and the new government dropped it. There is **no veto on a constitutional amendment bill**: the 24th Amendment of 1971 made assent compulsory.

For **state bills**, the Governor under Article 200 may assent, withhold, return or reserve the bill for the President. On a reserved bill, the President under Article 201 may assent, withhold, or direct the Governor to return it — but even if the legislature passes it again, the President is not bound to assent, and there is no time limit, so a pocket veto is possible here too.

| Bill | Central | State |
|---|---|---|
| Ordinary | Assent, reject or return | Assent, reject or return |
| Money | Assent or reject, not return | Assent or reject, not return |
| Constitutional amendment | Assent only | Cannot be introduced in a state legislature |81:T662,**Article 123** lets the President issue ordinances, with the force of an Act of Parliament, when Parliament is in recess. Four limits apply.

- An ordinance can be issued only when both Houses, or either of them, are not sitting; one made while both are sitting is void. It is not a parallel law-making power.
- The President must be satisfied that immediate action is needed. In **Cooper (1970)** the Court held that satisfaction can be challenged for mala fides — for instance, proroguing a House just to bypass it. The 38th Amendment shielded it; the 44th removed the shield.
- Its scope equals Parliament's legislative competence, and it faces the same limits, so it cannot abridge Fundamental Rights (Article 13 counts ordinances as "law").
- It must be laid before both Houses on reassembly and lapses **six weeks** after reassembly (counted from the later date if the Houses meet on different days), or sooner if both Houses disapprove. With a maximum gap of six months between sessions, an ordinance can survive at most six months and six weeks without approval. Acts done under a lapsed ordinance stay valid.

The President issues or withdraws ordinances only on ministerial advice. An ordinance may be retrospective, may amend Acts, ordinances or tax laws, but cannot amend the Constitution, and it needs no National Emergency. Lok Sabha rules require a statement explaining the urgency when a replacing bill is introduced. In **D. C. Wadhwa (1986)** the Court, reviewing Bihar's practice of repromulgating ordinances for years, held that repeated repromulgation without legislative action is a fraud on the Constitution.82:T526,**Article 72** lets the President grant clemency in three classes of case: offences against Union law, sentences by **court martial**, and all **death sentences**. The power is independent of the judiciary; the President does not sit as an appeal court, but can correct judicial error or soften a harsh sentence.

| Form | Effect |
|---|---|
| Pardon | Wipes out sentence and conviction and all disqualifications |
| Commutation | Swaps the punishment for a lighter kind (death to rigorous to simple imprisonment) |
| Remission | Shortens the sentence without changing its nature |
| Respite | Lighter sentence because of a special fact, such as disability or pregnancy |
| Reprieve | Temporary stay, especially of a death sentence, so clemency can be sought |

A Governor has a similar power under **Article 161** for offences against state law, but cannot touch court-martial sentences or pardon a death sentence, though a Governor may suspend, remit or commute one. In **Kehar Singh (1988)** the Court held there is no right to an oral hearing, the President may re-examine evidence, the power is exercised on cabinet advice, no guidelines are needed, and review is limited to decisions that are irrelevant, irrational, mala fide or discriminatory. **Maru Ram (1980)** had already held the advice and review points.83:T500,India adopted the British model, so the President is a **nominal executive**. Ambedkar compared the office to the English monarch: head of the State, not of the executive; the nation's symbol and seal, bound by ministerial advice, unable to dismiss ministers who hold a majority. Three articles fix the position: **Article 53** vests executive power in the President; **Article 74** provides a council of ministers headed by the PM to aid and advise; **Article 75** makes the council collectively responsible to the Lok Sabha.

The **42nd Amendment (1976)** made that advice expressly binding. The **44th (1978)** let the President send advice back once for reconsideration, after which the reconsidered advice binds. K. R. Narayanan used this twice: in October 1997 on a recommendation for President's Rule in Uttar Pradesh, which the cabinet then dropped, and in September 1998 for Bihar, imposed only in February 1999 after the cabinet repeated its advice.

There is no constitutional discretion, but there is **situational discretion**: choosing a Prime Minister when no party has a clear majority or a PM dies without an obvious successor, dismissing a council that cannot prove the Lok Sabha's confidence, and dissolving the Lok Sabha when the council has lost its majority.84:T45a,- Electoral college: elected MPs, elected MLAs, elected members of Delhi and Puducherry assemblies; no nominated members, no MLCs.
- MLA vote = state population (1971) ÷ elected MLAs ÷ 1,000; MP vote = total MLA votes ÷ elected MPs.
- PR by single transferable vote, secret ballot; quota = valid votes ÷ 2, plus 1; disputes go to the Supreme Court.
- Qualifications: citizen, 35 years, eligible for Lok Sabha, no office of profit; 50 proposers + 50 seconders; ₹15,000 deposit.
- Oath by the Chief Justice; resignation to the Vice-President; five-year term, unlimited re-election.
- Impeachment: one-fourth signatures, 14 days' notice, two-thirds of total membership in each House; never used.
- Vacancy election within six months; Vice-President, then Chief Justice, act meanwhile.
- Vetoes: absolute, suspensive and pocket — no qualified veto; none on amendment bills (24th Amendment).
- Ordinance (Art 123): only in recess, lapses six weeks after reassembly; maximum life six months six weeks.
- Pardon (Art 72): Union law, court martial, death sentence; Governor (Art 161) cannot pardon death sentences.85:T810,Article 75 says only that the President appoints the Prime Minister; it lays down no procedure. Convention fills the gap: the President invites the leader of the majority party in the Lok Sabha. The President is not free to pick anyone.

Real choice arises in two situations.

| Situation | What the President does | Example |
|---|---|---|
| No party has a clear majority in the Lok Sabha | Personal discretion; usually invites the leader of the largest party or coalition and asks for a vote of confidence within a month | 1979: President Neelam Sanjiva Reddy named Charan Singh after Morarji Desai's Janata government fell. This was the first use of the discretion |
| The sitting Prime Minister dies and there is no obvious successor | Individual judgement | 1984: after Indira Gandhi's assassination, President Zail Singh appointed Rajiv Gandhi rather than a caretaker; the party later elected him leader |

If the ruling party itself elects a new leader after a death, the President has no option but to appoint that person. After the deaths of Nehru and Shastri, when the leadership was contested, the seniormost minister, Gulzari Lal Nanda, was appointed to act as Prime Minister until the party chose its leader.

**Proving a majority.** In 1980 the Delhi High Court held that the Constitution does not demand that a person prove a majority before being appointed. The President may appoint first and ask for proof within a reasonable time. Charan Singh (1979), V. P. Singh (1989), Chandrasekhar (1990), P. V. Narasimha Rao (1991), A. B. Vajpayee (1996), Deve Gowda (1996), I. K. Gujral (1997) and Vajpayee again (1998) took office this way.

**Membership of a House.** In 1996 the Supreme Court held that a non-member can be made Prime Minister for six months, within which he or she must get into either House or cease to hold the post. The Prime Minister may belong to either House: Indira Gandhi (1966), Deve Gowda (1996) and Manmohan Singh (2004) were Rajya Sabha members. In Britain, by contrast, the Prime Minister must sit in the House of Commons.86:T57a,The President administers two oaths before the Prime Minister takes charge: the oath of office and the oath of secrecy. Both are in the same form as for any Union minister.

- **Oath of office** — faith and allegiance to the Constitution, upholding India's sovereignty and integrity, faithful discharge of duty, and fairness to all people according to the Constitution and law, free of fear, favour, affection or ill will.
- **Oath of secrecy** — not to reveal, directly or indirectly, any matter brought before or known to the Prime Minister as a Union minister, except as needed for official duty.

**Term.** The term is not fixed; the Prime Minister holds office during the pleasure of the President. That does not allow the President to dismiss the Prime Minister whenever he or she likes. While the Prime Minister commands a Lok Sabha majority, dismissal is not possible; once confidence is lost the Prime Minister must resign or can be dismissed. V. P. Singh (1990) and Deve Gowda (1997) resigned after losing votes in the Lok Sabha.

**Pay.** Parliament fixes the salary and allowances from time to time, under the Salaries and Allowances of Ministers Act, 1952. The Prime Minister draws what a member of Parliament receives, plus a sumptuary allowance, free housing, travel allowance and medical facilities. A 2001 amendment raised the sumptuary allowance from ₹1,500 to ₹3,000 a month.87:T501,| Relation to | Powers and duties |
|---|---|
| The President | Principal channel of communication with the council (Article 78): conveys all council decisions on Union administration and legislative proposals; supplies whatever information the President asks for; if asked, places before the council a matter a single minister has decided but the council has not considered |
| The President (advice) | Advises on appointing the Attorney General, the Comptroller and Auditor General, the chairman and members of the UPSC, election commissioners, and the chairman and members of the Finance Commission |
| Parliament | Leader of the Lower House; advises on summoning and proroguing sessions; may recommend dissolution of the Lok Sabha at any time; announces government policy on the floor of the House |

**Other roles.** The Prime Minister chairs NITI Aayog (successor to the Planning Commission), the National Integration Council, the Inter-State Council, the National Water Resources Council and some other bodies. Beyond that, the office shapes foreign policy, acts as chief spokesman of the Union government, is crisis manager at the political level during emergencies, meets delegations from different states, leads the ruling party and is the political head of the services.88:T569,The Constitution has no provision for a caretaker government; it is a situational arrangement and a practical necessity. The term is used for a council of ministers that has resigned, or whose house has been dissolved, and is asked to carry on until a successor takes over. Its main task is to hold free and fair elections.

Such a government is limited to routine administration. It should not announce major policy or start new schemes, except for national security or national interest.

- The Tarkunde Committee (1974–75), an unofficial electoral-reform body set up under Jayaprakash Narayan, said a caretaker government should not launch new policies, promise or begin projects, grant allowances, loans or pay rises, or hold official functions attended by ministers.
- In August 1979, while dissolving the Lok Sabha, President Reddy issued a communique recording the ministers' assurance of fair elections and no significant new policy or spending decisions, with urgent national-interest work excepted.
- The Calcutta High Court (December 1979) noted that the Constitution does not mention a caretaker government, but such an exceptional situation calls for one that only carries on day-to-day administration.
- Two reasons constrain a caretaker Prime Minister: the usual accountability to Parliament is missing, and the government must not use its position for electoral gain.89:T833,Under Article 79, Parliament has three parts: the **President**, the **Council of States** and the **House of the People** (Hindi names Rajya Sabha and Lok Sabha adopted in 1954). The President sits in neither House yet is integral to Parliament: no bill becomes law without assent, and the President summons and prorogues the Houses, dissolves the Lok Sabha, addresses them and issues ordinances during recess. This copies Britain's "Crown-in-Parliament"; the American President, by contrast, is no part of Congress.

| | Rajya Sabha | Lok Sabha |
|---|---|---|
| Maximum strength | 250 (238 from states and UTs + 12 nominated) | 550 (530 from states + 20 from UTs) |
| Present strength | 245 (225 states, 8 UTs, 12 nominated) | 543 (524 states, 19 UTs) |
| How chosen | Elected MLAs vote by proportional representation with single transferable vote (STV) | Direct election by adult voters in territorial constituencies |
| Term | Permanent; one-third retire every second year; six-year member terms | Five years from its first meeting, unless dissolved earlier |

Rajya Sabha seats follow population (Fourth Schedule), so Uttar Pradesh has 31 and Tripura one, unlike the two-per-state US Senate. Only three UTs (Delhi, Puducherry, Jammu and Kashmir) are represented there. The 12 nominees bring expertise in art, literature, science and social service without facing election. **Odisha** holds 10 Rajya Sabha and 21 Lok Sabha seats (3 reserved for SCs, 5 for STs).

The **61st Amendment (1988)** lowered the voting age from 21 to 18. Nomination of two Anglo-Indians to the Lok Sabha, last extended to 2020 by the 95th Amendment, was not renewed by the **104th Amendment (2019)** and lapsed on 25 January 2020.

The six-year Rajya Sabha term comes from the Representation of the People Act, 1951, not the Constitution; lots decided who in the first batch (1952) retired early. The President may dissolve the Lok Sabha before five years, and no court can question it. During a national emergency its life can be extended by law one year at a time, but not beyond six months after the emergency ends.8a:T4fc,Representation is kept uniform twice over: each state gets seats in proportion to population (states under six million excepted), and each state's constituencies carry roughly equal populations. Both are to be readjusted after every census by an authority Parliament sets up; Delimitation Commission Acts came in 1952, 1962, 1972 and 2002.

| Amendment | Effect |
|---|---|
| 42nd (1976) | Froze seat allocation and boundaries at the 1971 level till 2000 |
| 84th (2001) | Freeze extended to 2026; boundaries and reserved seats may be redrawn on 1991 figures |
| 87th (2003) | That redrawing to use the 2001 census, without changing seats per state |
| 104th (2019) | SC/ST reservation in the Lok Sabha extended to 2030 |

Reserved SC/ST seats are elected by all voters of the constituency; there is no separate electorate, and SC/ST candidates may also contest general seats.

The Lok Sabha uses **first-past-the-post** in single-member constituencies, which can under-represent minorities. India uses STV for the Rajya Sabha, legislative councils, and presidential and vice-presidential elections, but rejected it for the Lok Sabha: it is hard for voters to follow and tends to fragment parties. It is also costly, rules out by-elections and weakens the voter–member link.8b:T820,| | Constitution | Representation of the People Act, 1951 |
|---|---|---|
| Qualifications | Citizen; oath before a person authorised by the Election Commission; age **30** (Rajya Sabha) or **25** (Lok Sabha) | Registered elector in a parliamentary constituency; SC/ST to contest a reserved seat |
| Disqualifications | Office of profit (ministers and exempted posts excepted); unsound mind declared by a court; undischarged insolvent; foreign citizenship or allegiance | Corrupt practices; a sentence of two years or more (preventive detention excluded); unfiled election accounts; government contracts; office in a corporation with 25% government stake; dismissal for corruption; social crimes such as dowry or sati |

Since 2003 a Rajya Sabha candidate need not be an elector of the state concerned; the Supreme Court upheld this in 2006. The **President** decides disqualification disputes, acting on the **Election Commission's** opinion.

Under the **Tenth Schedule**, a member is unseated for leaving the party voluntarily, defying its whip, joining a party after winning as an independent, or (if nominated) joining one after six months. The **Speaker or Chairman** decides these cases, and the **Kihoto Hollohan** judgment (1992) made that decision reviewable by courts.

Seats are also vacated by:

- **double membership** — a person elected to both Houses has 10 days to choose, or loses the Rajya Sabha seat; one also elected to a state legislature loses the parliamentary seat unless the state seat is given up within 14 days;
- **resignation**, effective when the presiding officer accepts it as genuine;
- **absence** from every sitting for 60 days without leave;
- a void election, expulsion, or becoming President, Vice-President or Governor.

When a disqualified person wins, the 1951 Act lets the **High Court** void the election, with appeal to the Supreme Court. No member may vote or claim privileges before the oath; sitting without it costs ₹500 a day. Salaries are set by Parliament; a pension came in 1976, and in 2018 pay became ₹1,00,000 a month.8c:Ta45,The **Speaker** is chosen by the Lok Sabha from its members on a date the President fixes, and does not vacate office on dissolution, staying until the new House meets. Removal needs a resolution backed by a majority of all the then members, after 14 days' notice and with at least 50 members supporting its consideration; during that debate the Speaker cannot preside but may speak and vote in the first instance.

The Speaker's powers come from the Constitution, the Lok Sabha's rules and convention. The Speaker keeps order, is the final interpreter of rules and precedent within the House, adjourns for want of quorum, casts a vote only on a tie, presides at **joint sittings**, may allow a secret sitting, certifies **money bills** with final effect, decides **defection** cases, and chairs the Business Advisory, Rules and General Purposes Committees. Independence rests on removal only by effective majority, pay charged on the Consolidated Fund, protection from criticism except on a substantive motion, and seventh rank in precedence beside the Chief Justice. Unlike Britain, the Speaker does not resign from the party.

| Office | Key facts |
|---|---|
| Deputy Speaker | Elected after the Speaker on a date the Speaker fixes; not subordinate to the Speaker; heads any committee joined; often from the opposition |
