| Panel of chairpersons | Up to ten named by the Speaker; cannot preside if the offices are **vacant** (a member appointed by the President acts) |
| Speaker pro tem | Named by the President, usually the senior-most member; swears in members and conducts the Speaker's election |
| Chairman, Rajya Sabha | The Vice-President ex officio; not a member of the House; cannot vote while own removal is debated; no Chairman's pay while acting as President |
| Deputy Chairman | Elected by the Rajya Sabha; acts when the chair is vacant or the Vice-President acts as President |

The Chairman lacks two Speaker powers: certifying money bills and presiding at joint sittings. The offices began in 1921 as President and Deputy President of the Central Legislative Assembly; **Vithalbhai J. Patel** (1925) was the first Indian and elected holder. **G. V. Mavalankar** and **Ananthasayanam Ayyangar** were the first Speaker and Deputy Speaker of the Lok Sabha.

The **Leader of the House** is the Prime Minister if in the Lok Sabha, or a nominated minister. A Leader of the Opposition was first recognised in 1969 and given statutory status in 1977; recognition needs one-tenth of the House. **Whips** rest purely on convention; the government's chief whip is the Minister of Parliamentary Affairs in the Lok Sabha.8d:T5a7,The President summons the Houses, and the gap between sessions cannot exceed **six months**. Usually there are three: Budget (February–May), Monsoon (July–September) and Winter (November–December).

| | Adjournment | Prorogation | Dissolution |
|---|---|---|---|
| By | Presiding officer | President | President, or expiry |
| Ends | A sitting | The session | The Lok Sabha itself |
| Pending bills | Survive | Survive (notices lapse) | Mostly lapse |

On dissolution, a bill lapses if pending in the Lok Sabha or passed by it and pending in the Rajya Sabha. It survives if pending only in the Rajya Sabha, if a joint sitting was notified before dissolution, or if it awaits the President's assent or has come back from the President for reconsideration.

**Quorum** is one-tenth including the presiding officer: 55 in the Lok Sabha, 25 in the Rajya Sabha. Decisions go by majority of those present and voting, the presiding officer having only a casting vote. Divisions use the automatic recorder, "Aye"/"No" slips, or (now obsolete) lobbies. Business is in Hindi or English, with interpretation from Eighth Schedule languages; the Official Languages Act, 1963 kept English after 1965. **Ministers and the Attorney General** may speak in either House, at joint sittings and in their committees, but that right carries no vote. A **lame-duck session** is one held by an outgoing Lok Sabha after its successor is elected, as in 1957 and 1962.8e:T677,**Question hour** opens each sitting. Starred questions get oral answers with supplementaries; unstarred ones get written answers without; short-notice questions, on under ten days' notice, are answered orally. **Zero hour**, which follows, is not in the rules; it is an Indian innovation dating from 1962.

Closure motions end debate: simple, by compartments, **kangaroo** (only key clauses debated) and **guillotine** (leftover clauses voted when time expires).

| Device | Point to remember |
|---|---|
| Calling attention | Seeks a minister's statement on an urgent matter; Indian innovation of 1954, and in the rules |
| Adjournment motion | Interrupts business for a definite, urgent, recent matter; needs 50 members; implies censure, so **not used in the Rajya Sabha**; debate at least 2½ hours |
| No-confidence motion | Tests the ministry's majority (Art. 75); needs 50 members |
| Motion of thanks | On the President's address; defeat means defeat of the government |
| Point of order | Raised when rules are breached; suspends proceedings, no debate |
| Half-an-hour / short duration discussion | No formal motion or vote; up to three and two days a week respectively |
| Special mention | Rajya Sabha residual device; Lok Sabha's version is Rule 377 |

| Censure motion | No-confidence motion |
|---|---|
| Must state reasons | Needs no reasons |
| Against one minister, several or all | Against the whole council only |
| Ministry need not resign | Ministry must resign |

A **resolution** is always a substantive motion; private members' resolutions are taken on alternate Fridays, and statutory ones arise under the Constitution or an Act.8f:T9d0,Government (public) bills are moved by ministers on seven days' notice, and their defeat may topple the ministry; private members' bills need a month's notice and their defeat changes nothing. An **ordinary bill** goes through a **first reading** (introduction, no debate), a **second reading** (general discussion, committee stage, clause-by-clause consideration — the decisive stage), a **third reading** (accept or reject the whole, no amendments), the same steps in the **other House**, and **assent**. The President may assent, withhold assent, or return the bill once; if repassed, assent must follow, so the veto is only suspensive.

A **money bill** (Article 110) deals *only* with taxation, Union borrowing, the Consolidated and Contingency Funds, appropriation, charged expenditure, receipts and audit. Fines, licence or service fees and local taxes do not make a bill a money bill. The **Speaker's** certificate on this is final against courts, both Houses and the President.

| | Ordinary bill | Money bill |
|---|---|---|
| Introduced | Either House, by any member | Lok Sabha only, by a minister, on the President's recommendation |
| Rajya Sabha | Can amend or reject; may hold it six months | Only recommends; must return within **14 days** |
| Deadlock | Joint sitting possible | Not possible |
| President | Assent, withhold or return | Assent or withhold; cannot return |

Every money bill is a financial bill, but not vice versa. A **financial bill (I)** (Art. 117(1)) mixes Article 110 matters with general law; like a money bill it starts only in the Lok Sabha on the President's recommendation, but the Rajya Sabha may amend or reject it. A **financial bill (II)** (Art. 117(3)) involves spending from the Consolidated Fund with no Article 110 matter; it can start in either House, and the President's recommendation is needed at the **consideration** stage, not introduction.

A **joint sitting** (Article 108) settles deadlock on ordinary or financial bills — when the other House rejects a bill, final disagreement on amendments persists, or six months pass — never on money or amendment bills. It cannot be held on a bill that has lapsed, though one notified before dissolution survives. The **Speaker** presides, then the Deputy Speaker, then the Deputy Chairman; the Chairman never does. Lok Sabha rules apply and a simple majority passes the bill. Only three: **Dowry Prohibition (1960)**, **Banking Service Commission (Repeal) (1977)** and **Prevention of Terrorism (2002)**.90:T963,The Constitution never says "budget"; Article 112 calls it the **annual financial statement** for the year 1 April–31 March. The railway budget, separated in 1924 after the Acworth Committee, was merged back in **2017**.

| Article | Rule |
|---|---|
| 112 | Statement laid before both Houses; charged and voted spending shown apart |
| 113 | Demands only on the President's recommendation; only the Lok Sabha votes them and may cut but not raise them; charged items discussed, not voted |
| 114 | No withdrawal without an appropriation law |
| 116 | Vote on account, vote of credit, exceptional grants |
| 117 | Tax bills only on the President's recommendation and never in the Rajya Sabha |
| 265 | No tax without authority of law |

**Charged expenditure** includes the President's emoluments; pay of the presiding officers of both Houses; Supreme Court judges' salaries and pensions; High Court judges' **pensions**; the CAG and UPSC; debt charges; and court decrees.

The budget passes in six stages: presentation (on **1 February** since 2017, with no debate that day), general discussion (no cut motions or vote), scrutiny by the **24 departmental standing committees**, Lok Sabha voting on demands, the appropriation bill, and the finance bill. **Cut motions** come in three forms: policy cut (demand reduced to ₹1), economy cut (a specified sum) and token cut (₹100, to air a grievance). On the last day the Speaker puts all remaining demands to vote together — the **guillotine**.

A **vote on account**, usually two months' spending at about one-sixth of the total, bridges the gap until the Appropriation Act; it now accompanies the interim budget of an election year. Under the 1931 Provisional Collection of Taxes Act the finance bill must be enacted within **75 days**.

Other grants: **supplementary** (voted sum falls short), **additional** (new service), **excess** (overspending, voted after the year once the **Public Accounts Committee** approves), **vote of credit** (a "blank cheque" for an unforeseen demand), **exceptional** and **token** (funded by reappropriation).

The **Consolidated Fund** (Art. 266) takes all revenue and loans and needs a law for withdrawals; the **Public Account** (Art. 266) holds deposits and is run by executive action; the **Contingency Fund** (Art. 267) is at the President's disposal for unforeseen spending, held by the finance secretary.91:T4ab,Privileges protect each House, its committees and members, and extend to ministers and the Attorney General, but **not to the President**.

| Collective | Individual |
|---|---|
| Publish proceedings and bar others (the 44th Amendment let the press publish true reports, except of secret sittings) | No arrest during a session and 40 days either side — **civil** cases only |
| Secret sittings; own rules; punish breach or contempt | Freedom of speech in the House and its committees |
| No court inquiry into proceedings; no arrest within the precincts without the presiding officer's leave | Exemption from jury service and from testifying during a session |

Every breach of privilege is a contempt, but not every contempt is a breach. Article 105 first linked unlisted privileges to the British House of Commons; the 44th Amendment (1978) dropped that reference without changing substance. Privileges remain uncodified.

India's Parliament is not sovereign in Dicey's British sense. It is bound by a **written Constitution**, the **federal** division of powers, **judicial review**, and justiciable **Fundamental Rights** under Article 13 — closer to the US Congress than to Westminster.92:T868,The Constitution originally fixed the Court at eight judges: the Chief Justice and seven others. Article 124 lets Parliament raise the number, and it has done so six times. Today the sanctioned strength is 34 including the Chief Justice of India (CJI).

| Law | Judges other than CJI | Total with CJI |
|---|---|---|
| Article 124 (original) | 7 | 8 |
| Act of 1956 | 10 | 11 |
| Amendment Act of 1960 | 13 | 14 |
| Amendment Act of 1977 | 17 | 18 |
| Amendment Act of 1986 | 25 | 26 |
| Amendment Act of 2008 | 30 | 31 |
| Amendment Act of 2019 | 33 | 34 |

Judges are appointed by the President. For the Chief Justice, the President consults such Supreme Court and high court judges as are considered necessary. For every other judge, consulting the Chief Justice is compulsory, along with other judges as needed. What "consultation" really means has been settled by a series of Supreme Court rulings, called the Judges cases.

| Case | Year | What it held |
|---|---|---|
| First Judges case (S.P. Gupta) | 1981 | Consultation is only an exchange of views, not concurrence |
| Second Judges case (Advocates-on-Record Association) | 1993 | Consultation means concurrence; the CJI's advice binds the President, after consulting two senior colleagues; the senior-most judge must become CJI |
| Third Judges case (Presidential Reference) | 1998 | The CJI must consult a collegium of the four senior-most judges; a recommendation made without proper consultation does not bind the government |
| Fourth Judges case | 2015 | The 99th Amendment and the NJAC Act were struck down as hurting judicial independence; the collegium returned |

The 99th Amendment (2014) and the National Judicial Appointments Commission Act (2014) had tried to replace the collegium with a commission (Articles 124A to 124C). The 2015 verdict voided both. On the top post, the convention of promoting the senior-most judge held from 1950 to 1973, when A.N. Ray was chosen over three seniors (Shelat, Hegde, Grover) who had decided Kesavananda Bharati against the government; they resigned. In 1977 M.U. Beg was preferred over H.R. Khanna. The 1993 ruling closed this discretion.93:T4c2,Only the President can remove a judge, and only after both Houses, in the same session, pass an address supported by a special majority: a majority of the total membership of each House and at least two-thirds of those present and voting. There are two grounds, proved misbehaviour and incapacity. The word "impeachment" is not used in the Constitution for judges; it appears only for the President, though it is commonly used for judges too.

The Judges Enquiry Act, 1968 lays down the steps:

1. A notice of motion signed by 100 Lok Sabha members or 50 Rajya Sabha members goes to the Speaker or Chairman.
2. The presiding officer may admit or reject it.
3. If admitted, a three-member committee investigates: a Supreme Court judge (or the CJI), a high court Chief Justice and a distinguished jurist.
4. If the committee finds guilt or incapacity, the House may take up the motion.
5. After passage by special majority in each House, the address goes to the President, who orders removal.

No Supreme Court judge has ever been removed. The first case was that of Justice V. Ramaswami (1991–1993). The committee held him guilty of misbehaviour, but the motion failed in the Lok Sabha because the Congress abstained.94:T443,| Arrangement | Article | Who appoints | Condition |
|---|---|---|---|
| Acting Chief Justice | 126 | President | CJI post vacant, or CJI absent or unable to work |
| Ad hoc judge | 127 | CJI, after consulting the high court's Chief Justice and with the President's prior consent | Quorum of permanent judges is lacking; the person must be qualified to be a Supreme Court judge |
| Retired judge | 128 | CJI, with the consent of the President and of the person | Retired Supreme Court or qualified high court judge sits for a limited period; enjoys the powers of a judge but is not otherwise treated as one |

Delhi is the seat of the Court, but the CJI, with presidential approval, may name other places. This is a discretionary power, so no court can direct that it be used. With the President's approval the Court frames its own rules of procedure. Constitutional cases and Article 143 references go to a bench of at least five judges; other matters are heard by single judges or division benches. Judgments are delivered in open court, by majority, and dissenting opinions are permitted.95:T4d3,As guardian of the Constitution and protector of fundamental rights, the Court must be shielded from pressure by the executive and the legislature. The Constitution does so through the following safeguards.

- **Mode of appointment:** the executive acts only after consulting the judiciary itself.
- **Security of tenure:** removal only by the prescribed process and grounds, so judges do not serve at the President's pleasure.
- **Fixed service conditions:** pay and privileges cannot be reduced, save in a financial emergency.
- **Charged expenditure:** salaries, pensions and administrative costs fall on the Consolidated Fund of India, so Parliament may discuss but not vote on them.
- **No discussion of conduct:** neither Parliament nor a state legislature may debate a judge's conduct, except during a removal motion.
- **Ban on post-retirement practice:** retired judges cannot plead before any court or authority in India.
- **Contempt power:** the Court can punish those who lower its authority.
- **Own staff:** the CJI appoints officers and servants and fixes their service conditions without executive interference.
- **Protected jurisdiction:** Parliament cannot cut down the Court's jurisdiction, though it may widen it.96:T7ac,Alladi Krishnaswamy Ayyar of the Drafting Committee remarked that no other Supreme Court in the world has more powers.

**Original jurisdiction (Article 131).** It is exclusive in disputes between the Centre and states, or among states. Exclusive means no other court may hear the dispute, and original means it is heard first, not on appeal. The dispute must involve a legal right, so purely political questions are out, and a private person cannot sue the Centre or a state under this head. Four matters fall outside it: disputes arising from pre-Constitution treaties, agreements and sanads that still operate (or that bar the jurisdiction), inter-state river water disputes (under the 1956 Act), matters sent to the Finance Commission, and certain adjustments of expenses and pensions between the Centre and states. The first suit (1961) was West Bengal's challenge to the Coal Bearing Areas Act of 1957, which the Court dismissed.

**Writ jurisdiction (Article 32).** Any citizen whose fundamental rights are violated may go straight to the Supreme Court for habeas corpus, mandamus, prohibition, quo warranto or certiorari. This power is shared with high courts, so it is concurrent, unlike the exclusive federal jurisdiction. The Supreme Court issues writs only for fundamental rights, while a high court can do so for other purposes as well, so the high court's writ power is wider. Parliament may extend the Supreme Court's writ power to other purposes.

**Advisory jurisdiction (Article 143).** The President may refer two kinds of matter. For a question of law or fact of public importance, the Court may decline to answer. For a dispute arising from a pre-Constitution treaty or sanad, it must answer. Either way, the opinion is advisory and does not bind the President. Fifteen references have been made so far, from the Delhi Laws Act (1951) to the 2G spectrum matter (2012); others include Berubari Union (1960) and the Rama Janma Bhumi case (1993).97:Ta69,The Court mainly hears appeals from high courts, under four heads.

| Head | When an appeal lies |
|---|---|
| Constitutional | High court certifies that a substantial question of law on interpreting the Constitution is involved |
| Civil | High court certifies a substantial question of law of general importance that the Supreme Court should decide. The old ₹20,000 money limit went in 1972 (30th Amendment) |
| Criminal | As of right, without certificate, where the high court reversed an acquittal and awarded death, or took a case to itself and imposed death; the 1970 law added life imprisonment or ten years. Otherwise on a high court certificate. No appeal as of right if the high court reversed a conviction and acquitted |
| Special leave (Article 136) | Discretionary leave against any judgment of any court or tribunal except a military one; may be sought against interim or final orders, in any kind of matter |

**Court of record (Article 129).** Its proceedings are preserved as evidence and precedent, and it can punish contempt, including contempt of high courts, subordinate courts and tribunals (as held in 1991). The Contempt of Courts Act, 1971, drawn from the H.N. Sanyal committee (1963), divides contempt into civil (wilful disobedience of orders) and criminal (scandalising the court, prejudicing proceedings, obstructing justice). Fair criticism, innocent publication and fair reports are not contempt. Punishment is up to six months' simple imprisonment, a fine up to ₹2,000, or both, and proceedings must begin within a year. Village courts such as Nyaya Panchayats lie outside the Act.

**Judicial review.** The Court can declare Central and state laws and executive orders invalid if they violate the Constitution.

**Review and curative petitions (Article 137).** A review petition is filed within thirty days before the same bench, on grounds such as new evidence, an error on the face of the record or any sufficient reason. Even after dismissal, a curative petition is possible on narrow grounds such as breach of natural justice, a gross miscarriage of justice, abuse of process or bias.

**Other powers.** The Court alone decides disputes over the election of the President and Vice-President. On a presidential reference it inquires into misbehaviour by the UPSC chairman and members or their state counterparts, and its advice binds the President. It may withdraw cases from high courts or transfer them between high courts, its law binds all courts (Article 141), and it supervises all courts and tribunals. Parliament can enlarge its powers on Union List matters, and the Centre and states can do so by special agreement.98:T423,The Governor is not chosen by voters or by an electoral college. The President appoints by a warrant under hand and seal, so in effect the Governor is the Centre's nominee. In the Hargovind Pant case (1979), however, the Supreme Court held that the post is not employment under the Central government; it is an independent constitutional office, neither controlled by nor subordinate to the Centre.

The Draft Constitution proposed direct election on adult suffrage. The Constituent Assembly dropped this American model and adopted the Canadian one, in which the Centre appoints the provincial head. The reasons recorded include:

- election fits badly with a parliamentary state where the Governor is only a nominal head, and costly arrangements would serve no purpose;
- a popularly elected Governor could clash with the Chief Minister, would belong to a party instead of being neutral, and might encourage separatist tendencies;
- the Chief Minister might push a weak party loyalist as candidate;
- nomination lets the Centre keep its hold over the states.99:T69e,Only two qualifications are laid down: Indian citizenship and a minimum age of 35.

The **Sarkaria Commission** (1983–88) wanted more care in selection. A Governor should be eminent in some field, come from outside the state, be detached from local politics and not have been heavily active in politics lately. A politician of the Centre's ruling party should not be sent to a state run by another party. It proposed writing consultation with the Chief Minister into Article 155, and suggested that the Prime Minister may informally consult the Vice-President and the Lok Sabha Speaker.

**Oath.** The Governor promises to execute the office faithfully, to preserve, protect and defend the Constitution and the law, and to devote himself or herself to the people's service and well-being. It is administered by the Chief Justice of the state High Court, or the seniormost judge present. Anyone discharging the Governor's functions takes the same oath.

| Condition | Rule |
|---|---|
| Membership of legislature | Not allowed; a sitting member who is appointed is deemed to vacate the seat on taking office |
| Other office of profit | Barred |
| Residence | Raj Bhavan, without paying rent |
| Emoluments | Fixed by Parliament; cannot be reduced during the term |
| Two states | Pay shared between the states in a proportion the President decides |
| Salary | Raised in 2018 from ₹1.10 lakh to ₹3.50 lakh per month |

**Immunities.** The Governor has personal immunity for official acts. During the term, no criminal proceedings can be started, even for personal acts, and arrest or imprisonment is barred. Civil proceedings over personal acts are possible only after two months' notice.9a:T4b5,The Governor serves five years from entering office, but at the pleasure of the President, and may resign by writing to the President. In the Surya Narain case (1981) the Supreme Court held that the President's pleasure cannot be questioned in court, so the Governor has no security of tenure and can be removed at any time. The Constitution lists no grounds for removal.

That is why changes of government at the Centre often bring changes of Governors. In 1989 the National Front government under V. P. Singh asked all Governors to resign, because the Congress had appointed them; some were replaced and some stayed. In 1991 the Narasimha Rao government changed fourteen Governors appointed by the V. P. Singh and Chandra Sekhar governments.

- The President may transfer a Governor to another state for the remaining term.
- A Governor whose term has ended can be reappointed in the same or another state.
- A Governor continues beyond five years until the successor takes charge, so the state is never without one.
- For contingencies the Constitution does not cover, such as a Governor's death, the President may make arrangements; the Chief Justice of the High Court can be asked to act temporarily.9b:T52c,The Governor has executive, legislative, financial and judicial powers similar to the President's, but no diplomatic, military or emergency powers. Executive powers include:

- All executive action of the state is taken formally in the Governor's name; the Governor makes rules for authenticating orders, for convenient transaction of business and for allocating business among ministers.
- Appoints the Chief Minister and, on his or her advice, the other ministers, who hold office at the Governor's pleasure. Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh and Odisha must have a Tribal Welfare minister; the 94th Amendment (2006) removed Bihar from this list.
- Appoints the Advocate General (pleasure of the Governor, remuneration fixed by the Governor) and the State Election Commissioner, who can be removed only like a High Court judge.
- Appoints the chairman and members of the State Public Service Commission, though only the President can remove them.
- Can ask the Chief Minister for information on administration and legislative proposals, and can require a minister's individual decision to be placed before the council.
- Can recommend President's Rule; during that rule the Governor wields wide executive power as the President's agent.
- Acts as chancellor of the state's universities and appoints vice-chancellors.9c:T8a1,The Governor is an integral part of the state legislature and can:

- summon and prorogue it, and dissolve the legislative assembly;
- address it at the first session after each general election and at the first session of each year, and send messages on pending bills or otherwise;
- appoint a member to preside when the offices of both Speaker and Deputy Speaker, or both Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the council, are vacant;
- nominate one-sixth of the legislative council from people with special knowledge or practical experience in literature, science, art, the cooperative movement and social service;
- decide disqualification of members in consultation with the Election Commission;
- lay the reports of the State Finance Commission, the State Public Service Commission and the Comptroller and Auditor General before the legislature.

Until 2020 the Governor also nominated one Anglo-Indian member to the assembly; the 104th Amendment (2019) ended this.

**Assent to bills.** On receiving a bill passed by the legislature, the Governor has four choices: assent, withhold assent, return it for reconsideration (not a money bill), or reserve it for the President. If the legislature passes the returned bill again, with or without changes, the Governor must assent. Reservation is compulsory where the bill would endanger the position of the High Court. Soli Sorabji listed further grounds: a bill contrary to the Constitution or the Directive Principles, against the larger interest of the country, of grave national importance, or about compulsory acquisition of property under Article 31A.

**Ordinances (Article 213).** When the legislature is not in session, the Governor may issue ordinances; the legislature must approve them within six weeks of reassembling, and the Governor may withdraw one at any time. The text calls this the Governor's most important legislative power. In D. C. Wadhwa (1986) the Supreme Court found that Bihar's Governor had issued 256 ordinances between 1967 and 1981, many repromulgated again and again for one to fourteen years. Repeated repromulgation of the same text, without trying to pass a bill, violates the Constitution and the ordinance can be struck down.9d:T4fb,| Point | President | Governor |
|---|---|---|
| Ordinary bill | Assent, withhold or return; only a suspensive veto | Assent, withhold, return, or reserve for the President |
| Reserved bill | Can return it once; if passed again, not bound to assent, may withhold | Has no further role once reserved; the President's assent alone is needed |
| Money bill | Assent or withhold; cannot return | Assent, withhold or reserve; cannot return |
| Ordinance | When Parliament, or either House, is not in session | When the assembly, or either House of a bicameral legislature, is not in session |
| Ordinance scope | Subjects on which Parliament can legislate | Subjects on which the state legislature can legislate |
| Instructions | None needed | Needs the President's instructions in three cases: where a bill would need prior presidential sanction, where he would reserve such a bill, or where the Act would be invalid without presidential assent |
| Disapproval | Both Houses pass resolutions | Assembly resolution, agreed to by the council if there is one |
| Pardon: law | Offences against Central law | Offences against state law |
| Pardon: death sentence | Can pardon | Cannot pardon; can only suspend, remit or commute |
| Pardon: court-martial | Can act | No such power |9e:T881,Three Articles fix the position. **Article 154** vests the state's executive power in the Governor, exercisable directly or through subordinate officers. **Article 163** sets up a council with the Chief Minister at its head to aid and advise the Governor, except where the Governor must act in his or her discretion. **Article 164** makes the council collectively responsible to the legislative assembly, the basis of parliamentary government in the state.

The Governor differs from the President in two ways. The Constitution contemplates discretion for the Governor but not the President. And since the 42nd Amendment (1976), ministerial advice binds the President, while no such rule exists for the Governor. If a dispute arises over whether something falls within discretion, the Governor's decision is final and cannot be questioned in court.

**Constitutional discretion:**

- reserving a bill for the President;
- recommending President's Rule;
- acting as administrator of an adjoining union territory given as additional charge;
- fixing the royalty from mineral licences payable by Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram to an autonomous tribal district council.

**Situational discretion** arises from circumstances: choosing a Chief Minister when no party has a clear majority or when the incumbent dies with no obvious successor; dismissing a council that cannot prove confidence; and dissolving the assembly when the council has lost its majority.

**Special responsibilities.** Under presidential directions the Governor consults the ministers but finally acts in discretion in these cases:

| State | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| Maharashtra | Separate development boards for Vidarbha and Marathwada |
| Gujarat | Separate development boards for Saurashtra and Kutch |
| Nagaland | Law and order while disturbance continues in the Naga Hills–Tuensang area |
| Assam | Administration of tribal areas |
| Manipur | Administration of hill areas |
| Sikkim | Peace and social and economic advancement of different sections |
| Arunachal Pradesh | Law and order |
| Karnataka | Separate development board for the Hyderabad-Karnataka region (98th Amendment, 2012) |9f:T5f3,In January 1957 the Government of India appointed a committee under Balwantrai G. Mehta to study the Community Development Programme (1952) and the National Extension Service (1953). Reporting in November 1957, it proposed "democratic decentralisation", later known as Panchayati Raj.

- **Three tiers:** gram panchayat (village), panchayat samiti (block), zila parishad (district), linked by indirect elections.
- **Membership:** the village body directly elected; samiti and parishad indirectly elected.
- **Roles:** the samiti is the executive body; the parishad is advisory, coordinating and supervisory, with the Collector as chairman.
- **Substance:** all planning and development handed over, with real transfer of power and adequate resources.

The National Development Council accepted these ideas in January 1958 but let states choose their own patterns, provided the basic principles were common. Rajasthan was first: the Prime Minister inaugurated the scheme at Nagaur on 2 October 1959, and Andhra Pradesh followed the same year. States then diverged on tiers, tenure, composition and finance.

| Pattern | Example | Feature |
|---|---|---|
| Three-tier | Rajasthan | Samiti powerful because the block is the planning unit |
| Two-tier | Tamil Nadu | Only two levels |
| Four-tier | West Bengal | An extra level |
| Maharashtra–Gujarat | Maharashtra, Gujarat | Zila parishad powerful because the district is the planning unit |

Some states also set up nyaya panchayats to try petty civil and criminal cases.a0:T6d4,Many study teams examined particular aspects after 1960 (for example the Santhanam committees on finances and elections, and the Diwakar team on the Gram Sabha). Five committees shaped the road to constitutionalisation.

| Committee | Year | Core ideas |
|---|---|---|
| Ashok Mehta | 1977–78 (Janata government) | 132 recommendations; replace three tiers with two (zila parishad and mandal panchayat of 15,000–20,000 people); district as the base unit; compulsory taxation powers; social audit; elections within six months after supersession; seats for SCs and STs by population; constitutional recognition. Not implemented at the Centre as the government fell; Karnataka, West Bengal and Andhra Pradesh acted on parts of it |
| G.V.K. Rao | 1985–86 | Development had become bureaucratised, leaving panchayats "grass without roots"; zila parishad should be pivotal; a District Development Commissioner as its chief executive; regular elections (overdue for some tier in 11 states) |
| L.M. Singhvi | 1986 | New chapter in the Constitution; Gram Sabha as direct democracy; viable village units; more finances; tribunals for election and dissolution disputes |
| Thungon | 1988 | Three tiers; zilla parishad as pivot; five-year term; supersession for at most six months; state finance commission; list of subjects in the Constitution; reservation incl. women |
| Gadgil | 1988 | Constitutional status; direct election at all levels; reservation for SCs, STs and women; taxation powers; State Finance Commission and State Election Commission. Became the basis of the Amendment Bill |

The G.V.K. Rao report also differed from the Hanumantha Rao (1984) report, which preferred separate district planning bodies with the Collector as coordinator.a1:T4be,- **Gram Sabha (Article 243A):** all registered voters of the panchayat area; it performs the functions the state legislature gives it.
- **Three tiers:** village, intermediate and district. A state with a population not above 20 lakh need not create the intermediate tier.
- **Elections:** every member at all levels is directly elected. Chairpersons at the intermediate and district levels are chosen indirectly, from among the elected members; the village chairperson is chosen as the state law decides. All members, including chairpersons, vote in meetings.
- **Reservation for SCs and STs:** seats in proportion to their population in the panchayat area, at all three levels, plus reservation of chairperson posts as the state decides.
- **Reservation for women:** at least one-third of seats (including those within SC and ST quotas) and at least one-third of chairperson posts at every level.
- **Backward classes:** reservation is optional and left to the state legislature.
- **Limit on SC/ST reservation:** it lasts as long as the period in Article 334 (till 2030). The SC provisions do not apply in Arunachal Pradesh, which has no scheduled castes; this exemption was added by the 83rd Amendment (2000).a2:T7cd,| Subject | Rule |
|---|---|
| Term (243E) | Five years from first meeting; fresh elections before expiry, or within six months of dissolution. A panchayat formed after early dissolution serves only the remainder of the term; if less than six months remain, no election is needed |
| Disqualification (243F) | As for state legislature elections or under state law; but a person aged 21 or above cannot be barred for being under 25 |
| State Election Commission (243K) | Commissioner appointed by the Governor; removable only as a high court judge is; service conditions cannot be worsened after appointment |
| Powers (243G) | State law may devolve planning and implementation of schemes for economic development and social justice, including the 29 Eleventh Schedule subjects |
| Finance (243H) | State law may authorise taxes, duties, tolls and fees, assign state-levied ones, give grants-in-aid, and create panchayat funds |
| State Finance Commission (243-I) | Constituted by the Governor every five years; recommends sharing of tax proceeds, assignment of taxes, grants and ways to improve finances; its report with an action-taken note goes to the legislature. The Union Finance Commission also suggests measures to supplement state funds for panchayats |
| Audit (243J) | State law governs accounts and audit |
| Union territories (243L) | Part applies, but the President may modify it |
| Exempted areas (243M) | Nagaland, Meghalaya, Mizoram, Fifth and Sixth Schedule (scheduled and tribal) areas, hill areas of Manipur with district councils, and Darjeeling district of West Bengal. Parliament may extend the Part to scheduled and tribal areas |
| Existing laws (243N) | Continued for one year from commencement, so states had to conform by 24 April 1994; existing panchayats ran out their term unless dissolved |
| Courts (243-O) | Courts cannot question laws on delimitation or seat allotment; elections can be challenged only by election petition before the authority the state names |a3:T80c,Part IX does not automatically apply to Fifth Schedule areas, but Parliament may extend it with modifications. It did so through the **Provisions of the Panchayats (Extension to the Scheduled Areas) Act, 1996**, known as PESA or the Extension Act (assent: 24 December 1996). Ten states have Fifth Schedule areas: Andhra Pradesh, Telangana, Chhattisgarh, Gujarat, Himachal Pradesh, Jharkhand, Madhya Pradesh, Maharashtra, **Odisha** and Rajasthan. All have amended their panchayat laws to comply.

The Act aims at self-rule for tribal people, makes the Gram Sabha the centre of village affairs, fits administration to traditional practice, protects tribal customs, and stops higher-level panchayats from taking over lower-level powers.

- **Customary law:** state legislation must respect custom, religious practice and traditional management of community resources.
- **Gram Sabha role:** it approves development plans before the panchayat takes them up, identifies beneficiaries of poverty alleviation schemes, and certifies fund use.
- **Reservation:** seats are proportional to population but STs get at least half, and all chairperson posts at every level go to STs. The state may nominate unrepresented STs to intermediate and district panchayats up to one-tenth of elected members.
- **Land and projects:** the Gram Sabha or panchayat must be consulted before land acquisition and resettlement; minor water bodies are managed by panchayats.
- **Minor minerals:** recommendation of the Gram Sabha or panchayat is mandatory for prospecting licences and mining leases, and prior recommendation for auctions of concessions.
- **Specific powers:** enforce or restrict intoxicants, own minor forest produce, prevent land alienation and restore unlawfully alienated tribal land, manage village markets, control money-lending to STs, supervise social-sector institutions, and control local plans including tribal sub-plans.
- **Model:** the Sixth Schedule pattern is to be followed for district-level arrangements. Inconsistent laws lapsed one year after assent.a4:T53f,Panchayats draw funds from Union grants (Central Finance Commission, Article 280), State Finance Commission devolution (Article 243-I), state loans and grants, Centrally Sponsored Schemes and their own tax and non-tax revenue. The Second Administrative Reforms Commission found that own revenue is thin, dependence on grants is heavy, and much of the grant money is tied to schemes. Village panchayats hold most tax powers (property, profession, land, vehicles, entertainment, water, lighting); the higher tiers rely on tolls, fees and non-tax items. The result is responsibility without matching resources.

Reasons commonly given for weak performance:

- inadequate devolution of the "3Fs": functions, funds and functionaries;
- bureaucratic control that makes sarpanches run to block offices;
- tied funds that do not suit local needs;
- over-dependence on government money, which reduces demand for social audit;
- reluctance to levy taxes on one's own neighbours;
- Gram Sabha powers left unspecified in many state Acts;
- parallel bodies and missions that bypass panchayats;
- poor infrastructure, no full-time secretary, and poorly trained, often semi-literate representatives.

After constitutionalisation further bodies studied the system, such as the Lalit Mathur task force (2001) and the Mani Shankar Aiyar expert committee (2012).a5:T453,- Local government is a State List subject (entry 5); Part IX (Articles 243 to 243-O) was added by the 73rd Amendment, in force from 24 April 1993.
- Balwantrai Mehta (1957): three tiers, democratic decentralisation; Rajasthan (Nagaur, 2 October 1959) first.
- Ashok Mehta proposed two tiers; G.V.K. Rao coined "grass without roots"; Singhvi and Thungon and Gadgil pushed constitutional status.
- Gram Sabha is all registered voters; three tiers, except small states under 20 lakh may skip the middle tier.
- All members directly elected; intermediate and district chairpersons indirectly elected.
- One-third of seats and chairperson posts for women; SC/ST seats by population; backward-class quota optional.
- Five-year term; fresh election within six months of dissolution; minimum age 21.
- State Election Commission and State Finance Commission (every five years) are compulsory.
- Eleventh Schedule has 29 subjects; Part IX exempts Nagaland, Meghalaya, Mizoram and Scheduled and tribal areas.
- PESA 1996 extends the system to Fifth Schedule areas, including Odisha, with the Gram Sabha at the centre.a6:T575,Article 324 provides that the Commission has a Chief Election Commissioner (CEC) and as many other election commissioners as the President may fix from time to time. Both the CEC and the other commissioners are appointed by the President. If other commissioners are appointed, the CEC acts as chairman. The President may also appoint regional commissioners, after consulting the Commission, to assist it. Service conditions and tenure are determined by the President, subject to any law of Parliament.

The body's size has changed over time.

| Period | Position |
|---|---|
| 1950 to 15 October 1989 | Single-member body: only the CEC |
| 16 October 1989 | Two more commissioners appointed because the voting age had been lowered from 21 to 18 (61st Amendment, 1988); a three-member body |
| January 1990 | The two posts abolished; back to a single member |
| October 1993 | Two commissioners appointed again; a three-member body since then |

The Election Commission (Conditions of Service of Election Commissioners and Transaction of Business) Act, 1991 adds the following. The CEC and the two commissioners have equal powers and receive pay, allowances and perquisites like those of a Supreme Court judge. Differences of opinion are settled by majority vote of the Commission. The term is six years or until age 65, whichever comes first, and any member can resign by writing to the President.a7:T4fc,Its authority over elections to Parliament, state legislatures and the offices of President and Vice-President falls into three groups.

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| Administrative | Fix constituency boundaries under the Delimitation Commission Acts; prepare and revise electoral rolls and register voters; announce election dates and scrutinise nominations; supervise the election machinery; request staff from the President or Governor; cancel polls in case of rigging, booth capturing or violence; prepare a roster for party broadcasts on radio and TV |
| Advisory | Advise the President on disqualification of MPs; advise the Governor on disqualification of state legislators; advise the President whether elections can be held in a state under President's Rule so that an Emergency can be extended beyond one year |
| Quasi-judicial | Settle disputes about recognition of political parties and allotment of symbols; appoint officers to enquire into disputes about electoral arrangements |

Other functions include recognising political parties, giving them national or state status according to poll performance, allotting symbols, and framing the code of conduct for parties and candidates. Gandhi issued the call of Do or Die. The Congress passed the Quit India resolution on 8 August 1942, and Gandhi was arrested on 9 August 1942. The speed of arrest after the resolution is part of the factual sequence: resolution one day, arrest the next. The campaign was not a repeat of the Dandi march with a new name. Dandi was organised around a specific law breach in 1930; Quit India demanded the immediate withdrawal of British rule in 1942 and was launched when the leadership was removed from the field within hours.\n\nFor Prelims, the safest anchors are the call, the resolution date, and the arrest date, kept in that order. This single fact eliminates any option that asserts a decipherment. The 2025 OPSC Prelims match-the-columns item on the language of the Indus Valley Civilisation, which pairs hypotheses associated with a Dravidian hypothesis, S. R. Rao, R. Kinnier-Wilson and Asko Parpola, and Soviet scholars, tests recognition of the scholarly debate rather than knowledge of a deciphered text. Reasoning patterns for Paper II — statements, series, relations, puzzles and analytical tests. Build steady Paper II reasoning judgment for statements, series, relations and puzzles, and avoid the hurried assumptions that cost marks in a qualifying paper.","anchor":""},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"What Paper II reasoning tests","text":"Reasoning ability in Paper II covers statement-based questions that test factual inference, analytical ability tasks that test ordered thinking, and reasoning tasks involving series, relationships and puzzles as applicable. Examiners mix the three families so that a candidate who is strong only in one family still faces unfamiliar formats. The safe preparation is therefore structural: learn to distinguish what a statement asserts from what merely follows, to test each option independently, and to record assumptions explicitly before selecting an answer.\n\nThree habits carry over to every family. First, translate words into form: who is where, what follows what, which condition excludes which. Second, test the extremes: if an option claims always or never, check it against a single counterexample. Third, do not import outside facts into a statement question. A statement may be true in the world yet still not follow from the given passage, and following is the only question asked.","anchor":"what-paper-ii-reasoning-tests"},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"Statement-based reasoning: correct versus follows","text":"$2d","anchor":"statement-based-reasoning-correct-versus-follows"},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"Series, analogy and relationship tasks","text":"$2e","anchor":"series-analogy-and-relationship-tasks"},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"Analytical puzzles, coding and blood relations","text":"$2f","anchor":"analytical-puzzles-coding-and-blood-relations"},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"Odisha angle: qualifying pressure and steady coverage","text":"Paper II is qualifying for OPSC at 33 percent marks, and all OPSC aspirants engage with Logical Reasoning as a Paper II topic. The Odisha angle is practical rather than factual: the qualifying threshold rewards steady coverage and restraint. A candidate who secures reasoning marks through careful elimination is safer than one who chases uncertain hard items while leaving easy analytic items unattempted. Negative marking and exact question counts are not stated in the scheme and remain needs verification, so time discipline should be built around accuracy first.","anchor":"odisha-angle-qualifying-pressure-and-steady-coverage"},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"Revise in 60 seconds","text":"- Paper II is qualifying at 33 percent; reasoning covers statements, analysis, series and relations.\n- Correct means supported by the passage; follows means unavoidable consequence.\n- Mark statements supported, contradicted or unsupported before deciding.\n- Test the simplest series rule first; watch for interleaved series.\n- Match the relation in analogies, not the topic vocabulary.\n- Place the most restrictive puzzle condition first and eliminate violations.","anchor":"revise-in-60-seconds"},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"Common exam traps","text":"- Which statements are correct is confused with which conclusion follows; the tests are different.\n- Combining statements I and II without independent support is incorrect; each must be supported first.\n- Choosing the plausible general truth over the passage-supported statement is incorrect in statement tasks.\n- Forcing the first series rule that looks familiar is incorrect; test it on every visible term.\n- Assuming a missing puzzle detail or a possible arrangement as the only one is incorrect.","anchor":"common-exam-traps"},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"Related topics","text":"Comprehension · General Mental Ability · Decision Making and Problem Solving · Basic Numeracy and Data Interpretation · Analytical Ability","anchor":"related-topics"},{"topicId":"aptitude.logical-reasoning","topic":"Logical Reasoning and Analytical Ability","title":"Paper II qualifying","text":"What is Paper II and what score qualifies? Granville Austin credited this group with setting India on the road to its Constitution, noting that its draft closely resembled the Objectives Resolution.","anchor":"the-congress-experts-committee"},{"topicId":"polity.making-of-the-constitution","topic":"Making of the Constitution","title":"Criticisms of the Assembly","text":"| Charge | The critics' argument |\n|---|---|\n| Not representative | Members were not chosen directly by the people on universal adult franchise |\n| Not sovereign | It was created by British proposals and sat with British permission |\n| Too slow | The American framers needed only about four months; Naziruddin Ahmed mocked the Drafting Committee as a \"Drifting Committee\" |\n| Congress-dominated | Granville Austin called it a one-party body in an essentially one-party country |\n| Lawyers and politicians | Other groups were under-represented, which critics blame for the bulky, complex text |\n| Hindu-dominated | Viscount Simon called it a body of Hindus; Churchill said it spoke for one major community only |\n\nFor comparison, the USA took less than four months, Canada about two and a half years, Australia about nine years and South Africa one year.","anchor":"criticisms-of-the-assembly"},{"topicId":"polity.making-of-the-constitution","topic":"Making of the Constitution","title":"Other facts and the Hindi text","text":"| Role | Person or item |\n|---|---|\n| Seal of the Assembly | The elephant |\n| Constitutional (legal) adviser | Sir B. Grouped for revision, it covers:\n\n| Area | What a constitution may do |\n|---|---|\n| The community | Mark out the boundaries of the political community and state the nature and source of its authority |\n| Identity | Express the identity and values of the nation |\n| Citizens | Set out the rights and duties of citizens |\n| Institutions | Create and regulate political institutions |\n| Power-sharing | Divide or share power among levels of government or sub-national communities |\n| Religion | State any official religious identity and settle how sacred and secular authority relate |\n| Goals | Commit the state to particular social, economic or developmental aims |\n\nNotice that the last item is optional: not every constitution sets goals, which leads to Bulmer's distinction between procedural and prescriptive types below.","anchor":"functions-of-a-constitution"},{"topicId":"polity.concept-of-the-constitution","topic":"Concept of the Constitution","title":"Qualities of a good constitution","text":"Seven qualities are usually listed. Each has a reason attached, which is what examiners test.\n\n| Quality | Why it matters |\n|---|---|\n| Brevity | Unneeded detail makes clauses hard to interpret |\n| Clarity | Plain language aids understanding |\n| Definiteness | Provisions with one settled meaning limit judicial discretion |\n| Comprehensiveness | Spelling out government powers and citizens' rights and duties cuts down disputes and litigation |\n| Suitability | The text should fit the nation's history, society, economy and politics, and its people's aspirations |\n| Stability | Resistance to casual tampering builds political stability and citizens' respect for the document |\n| Adaptability | It should be a living, dynamic text that can respond to change |\n\nThere is a built-in tension here: stability argues for making change difficult, while adaptability argues for making it possible. The Indian answer to that tension is its mixed amendment procedure.","anchor":"qualities-of-a-good-constitution"},{"topicId":"polity.concept-of-the-constitution","topic":"Concept of the Constitution","title":"Evolved vs enacted, written vs unwritten","text":"$3c","anchor":"evolved-vs-enacted-written-vs-unwritten"},{"topicId":"polity.concept-of-the-constitution","topic":"Concept of the Constitution","title":"Rigid vs flexible","text":"This classification, based on the **method of amendment**, comes from **Lord James Bryce**.\n\n| Point | Rigid | Flexible |\n|---|---|---|\n| Amendment | Needs a special procedure, unlike ordinary laws | Changed like any ordinary law |\n| Constitutional vs ordinary law | Kept distinct | No distinction |\n| Other name | Inelastic | Elastic |\n| Examples | USA, Australia, Switzerland | UK, New Zealand |\n\nIndia sits in between: its Constitution is a **synthesis of rigidity and flexibility**, neither purely one nor the other.","anchor":"rigid-vs-flexible"},{"topicId":"polity.concept-of-the-constitution","topic":"Concept of the Constitution","title":"Federal vs unitary","text":"The basis here is how the national and regional governments relate.\n\n| Point | Federal | Unitary |\n|---|---|---|\n| Power | Divided between national and regional governments | Concentrated in the national government |\n| Regional units | Act independently in their own sphere | Act as subordinate agents using only delegated powers |\n| Examples | USA, Switzerland, Australia, Canada, Russia, Brazil | UK, France, Japan, China, Italy, Norway |\n\nIndia's Constitution mixes federal and unitary provisions, which is why **K. C. Wheare** called it **quasi-federal**.","anchor":"federal-vs-unitary"},{"topicId":"polity.concept-of-the-constitution","topic":"Concept of the Constitution","title":"Procedural vs prescriptive","text":"Elliot Bulmer also identifies two broad archetypes, based on what the document is for.\n\n- A **procedural** constitution concentrates on structure: it lays out the legal and political shape of public institutions and the legal limits on state power, so as to protect democratic processes and basic human rights.\n- A **prescriptive** constitution does all of that and more: it presumes, or tries to establish, a wide agreement on shared social goals that public authorities are bound to pursue.\n\nA constitution that commits the state to social and economic objectives, as the Directive Principles do, leans towards the prescriptive model.","anchor":"procedural-vs-prescriptive"},{"topicId":"polity.concept-of-the-constitution","topic":"Concept of the Constitution","title":"Constitutionalism and constitutional government","text":"$3d","anchor":"constitutionalism-and-constitutional-government"},{"topicId":"polity.concept-of-the-constitution","topic":"Concept of the Constitution","title":"Revise in 60 seconds","text":"- \"Constitution\" comes from the Latin constituere, to establish; India combines the two. **Article 368** offers two routes: a **special majority** of Parliament — two-thirds of members present and voting in each House plus a majority of each House's total membership — and, for certain provisions, that special majority **plus ratification by half the states**. A third set of provisions can be changed by a **simple majority** through the ordinary law-making process; such changes fall outside Article 368 altogether.","anchor":"rigid-yet-flexible"},{"topicId":"polity.salient-features","topic":"Salient Features of the Constitution","title":"Federal system with a unitary bias","text":"| Federal features | Unitary (non-federal) features |\n|---|---|\n| Two sets of government | Strong Centre |\n| Division of powers | Single constitution |\n| Written constitution | Single citizenship |\n| Supremacy of the constitution | Flexibility of the constitution |\n| Rigidity of the constitution | Integrated judiciary |\n| Independent judiciary | Governors appointed by the Centre |\n| Bicameralism | All-India services; emergency provisions |\n\nThe word \"federation\" appears nowhere in the text. **Article 1** calls India a **\"Union of States\"**, meaning the federation did not arise from an agreement among states, and no state may secede. Hence the many labels:\n\n| Description | Given by |\n|---|---|\n| Federal in form, unitary in spirit | (general description) |\n| Quasi-federal | K. C. In the USA, by contrast, federal courts apply federal law and state courts apply state law.\n\nThe Supreme Court is at once a federal court, the final court of appeal, the guarantor of citizens' fundamental rights and the guardian of the Constitution. Its independence is protected by security of tenure and fixed service conditions for judges, the charging of its expenses on the Consolidated Fund of India, a bar on legislatures discussing judges' conduct, a ban on practice after retirement, its own power to punish contempt, and the separation of the judiciary from the executive.","anchor":"an-integrated-and-independent-judiciary"},{"topicId":"polity.salient-features","topic":"Salient Features of the Constitution","title":"Rights, Directive Principles and Duties","text":"$41","anchor":"rights-directive-principles-and-duties"},{"topicId":"polity.salient-features","topic":"Salient Features of the Constitution","title":"A secular state","text":"$42","anchor":"a-secular-state"},{"topicId":"polity.salient-features","topic":"Salient Features of the Constitution","title":"Universal adult franchise and single citizenship","text":"$43","anchor":"universal-adult-franchise-and-single-citizenship"},{"topicId":"polity.salient-features","topic":"Salient Features of the Constitution","title":"Independent bodies and emergency provisions","text":"$44","anchor":"independent-bodies-and-emergency-provisions"},{"topicId":"polity.salient-features","topic":"Salient Features of the Constitution","title":"Three-tier government and co-operative societies","text":"Like other federations, the Constitution originally set up only two levels. The 1992 amendments added a constitutionally recognised third, local tier — found in no other constitution.\n\n| Amendment (1992) | Added | Structure |\n|---|---|---|\n| 73rd | Part IX and the 11th Schedule — **panchayats** | Village, intermediate and district levels in every state |\n| 74th | Part IX-A and the 12th Schedule — **municipalities** | Nagar panchayat (transitional area), municipal council (smaller urban area), municipal corporation (larger urban area) |\n\nThe **97th Amendment Act of 2011** gave co-operative societies constitutional standing in three ways: forming them became a fundamental right under **Article 19**; a new Directive Principle, **Article 43-B**, asks the state to promote them; and a new **Part IX-B** (Articles 243-ZH to 243-ZT) was added to keep them democratic, professional, autonomous and financially sound. In a monarchy the head of State holds office by heredity, as in Britain. In a republic the head of State is elected, directly or indirectly, for a fixed term, as in the USA. India's head of State is the President, elected indirectly for five years. A republic also implies that political sovereignty rests with the people rather than one person, and that no privileged class exists, so public office is open to every citizen without discrimination.","anchor":"republic"},{"topicId":"polity.preamble","topic":"Preamble of the Constitution","title":"Justice, Liberty, Equality and Fraternity","text":"$48","anchor":"justice-liberty-equality-and-fraternity"},{"topicId":"polity.preamble","topic":"Preamble of the Constitution","title":"Why the Preamble matters","text":"The Preamble sums up the philosophy and values behind the Constitution. Kesavananda (1973) and LIC (1995): part of the Constitution.\n- It is non-justiciable, and amendable only without touching basic features.","anchor":"revise-in-60-seconds"},{"topicId":"polity.preamble","topic":"Preamble of the Constitution","title":"Common exam traps","text":"- \"Socialist\" and \"Secular\" were part of the original 1949 text is incorrect; they were added in 1976, although the ideas were implicit earlier.\n- The Preamble is not a part of the Constitution is incorrect today; that was the Berubari view, overruled in Kesavananda Bharati.\n- The Preamble cannot be amended is incorrect; it can be, if basic features stay intact, and it has been amended once.\n- The Preamble can be enforced in a court of law is incorrect; it is non-justiciable and is neither a source nor a limit of legislative power.\n- Commonwealth membership reduces India's sovereignty is incorrect; it is a voluntary association that can be ended.\n- The ideal of liberty, equality and fraternity comes from the Russian Revolution is incorrect; that is the French Revolution, while justice is traced to Russia.","anchor":"common-exam-traps"},{"topicId":"polity.preamble","topic":"Preamble of the Constitution","title":"Related topics","text":"Making of the Constitution · Salient Features of the Constitution · Fundamental Rights · Directive Principles · Amendment of the Constitution","anchor":"related-topics"},{"topicId":"polity.preamble","topic":"Preamble of the Constitution","title":"Key words of the Preamble","text":"Which words describe the State and which describe the aims? Parliament does the rest.\n- Citizenship Act 1955: five ways of acquiring, three of losing.\n- India has single citizenship; the USA and Australia have double citizenship.\n- Birth rule: before 1 July 1987 any birth; 1987–2004 one citizen parent; after 3 December 2004 both parents, or one with the other not an illegal migrant.\n- Naturalisation: twelve months plus eleven of the preceding fourteen years; reduced to five for specified CAA 2019 migrants.\n- Loss: renunciation, termination (acquiring foreign citizenship), deprivation (by the Government).\n- Article 9: voluntarily acquiring foreign citizenship ends Indian citizenship; dual citizenship is not allowed.\n- OCI is not dual citizenship, and OCI holders cannot vote or hold constitutional offices.\n- CAA 2019 came into force on 10 January 2020; cut-off date 31 December 2014.\n- The Commonwealth citizenship provisions were removed in 2003; the Constitution forbids dual citizenship and OCI holders cannot vote or hold high office.\n- Article 11 is about acquisition at commencement is incorrect; Articles 5 to 8 deal with commencement, whereas Article 11 gives Parliament its law-making power.\n- Termination and deprivation are the same is incorrect; Because it says \"section of citizens\", it is not limited to minorities.\n\n**Article 30** is confined to minorities, religious or linguistic, though \"minority\" is not defined in the Constitution. Minorities may set up and run educational institutions of their choice and teach in their own language; the State may not discriminate against them in giving aid; and compensation for acquiring a minority institution's property must not defeat the right (added in 1978). Aided or recognised institutions accept regulation of syllabus, standards and staff. The **Malankara Syrian Catholic College case (2006)** held that the right secures equality with the majority, not an advantage over it, and gives no licence to maladminister.","anchor":"cultural-and-educational-rights-articles-29-and-30"},{"topicId":"polity.fundamental-rights","topic":"Fundamental Rights","title":"Remedies: Articles 32 and 226 compared","text":"$5e","anchor":"remedies-articles-32-and-226-compared"},{"topicId":"polity.fundamental-rights","topic":"Fundamental Rights","title":"Armed forces, martial law and Article 35","text":"**Article 33** lets Parliament — not state legislatures — restrict or abrogate the rights of the armed forces, police, intelligence agencies and similar services to keep discipline; such laws (the Army Act, 1950, for instance) cannot be challenged under Part III and also cover non-combatant staff.\n\n**Article 34** lets Parliament indemnify those who restored order in a martial-law area and validate sentences passed there. Martial law is undefined and only implied by Article 34, and it does not by itself suspend habeas corpus. Unlike a National Emergency under Article 352, it covers a specific area, affects only Fundamental Rights and displaces ordinary government and courts.\n\n**Article 35** gives Parliament alone the laws under Articles 16(3), 32, 33 and 34, and obliges it to set punishments for untouchability and trafficking, even on State List subjects.","anchor":"armed-forces-martial-law-and-article-35"},{"topicId":"polity.fundamental-rights","topic":"Fundamental Rights","title":"Property and the saving clauses: Articles 31A, 31B and 31C","text":"$5f","anchor":"property-and-the-saving-clauses-articles-31a-31b-and-31c"},{"topicId":"polity.fundamental-rights","topic":"Fundamental Rights","title":"Criticism, significance and rights outside Part III","text":"Critics point to excessive exceptions (Jaspat Roy Kapoor wanted the chapter renamed after its limitations), missing social and economic rights such as work and social security, vague terms (Sir Ivor Jennings's \"paradise for lawyers\", with no consistent philosophy), amendability, suspension in emergency, costly litigation and preventive detention. Even so, the rights are the bedrock of Indian democracy: they guard liberty, the rule of law, minorities, secularism and personal dignity.\n\nJusticiable rights **outside Part III** — Article 265 (tax only by law), 300A (property), 301 (free trade and commerce) and 326 (adult suffrage) — cannot be enforced under Article 32; T. Shah called the directives \"pious superfluities\" and compared them to a cheque payable only when the bank has funds. Nasiruddin likened them to new-year resolutions broken by 2 January, and T. T. Krishnamachari called them a \"veritable dustbin of sentiments\". K. C. Wheare described them as a \"manifesto of aims and aspirations\", and Ivor Jennings as \"pious aspirations\".\n- **Illogical arrangement.** N. Srinivasan complained that they are neither classified nor arranged sensibly, mixing minor matters with vital ones.\n- **Conservative philosophy.** Jennings saw in them nineteenth-century English thought, \"Fabian socialism without the socialism\".\n- **Risk of conflict.** K. Santhanam foresaw tension between Centre and states, President and Prime Minister, and Governor and Chief Minister over compliance.","anchor":"criticism"},{"topicId":"polity.directive-principles","topic":"Directive Principles of State Policy","title":"Value of the directives","text":"Defenders reply that the directives are no mere appendage. L. M. Singhvi called them the life-giving provisions of the Constitution, and Ambedkar valued them because they set economic democracy, not only political democracy, as the goal. Granville Austin said they aim to further the goals of the social revolution, and B. N. Rau saw at least an educative value.\n\nM. C. Setalvad listed their uses: they act as general instructions to every authority, serve as beacon-lights for judicial review, form the background to State action and amplify the Preamble. Parliament is nevertheless free to give them effect through ordinary laws.","anchor":"features"},{"topicId":"polity.fundamental-duties","topic":"Fundamental Duties","title":"Criticism","text":"- **Incomplete list.** Duties such as voting, paying taxes and family planning are absent, even though the Swaran Singh Committee had suggested the tax duty.\n- **Vagueness.** Expressions like \"noble ideals\", \"composite culture\" and \"scientific temper\" can be read in many ways, and an ordinary citizen may not follow them. D. D. Chawla made this point sharply.\n- **Moral code only.** Without legal sanction they are exhortations, a striking contrast with the committee's own proposal of penalties.\n- **Superfluous.** C. K. Daphtary argued that almost every citizen is law-abiding and does not need to be told duties; A. K. Sen compared the State to a harsh schoolmaster scolding pupils who have not done their homework.\n- **Placement.** Critics said Part IVA, tucked after Part IV, lowers their status, and that they ought to follow Part III to stand level with rights.","anchor":"criticism"},{"topicId":"polity.fundamental-duties","topic":"Fundamental Duties","title":"Significance","text":"Supporters value the duties for four reasons. They remind citizens that rights come with obligations to country, society and fellow citizens. They warn against acts such as burning the National Flag or damaging public property. They inspire discipline and make citizens active participants rather than spectators. And they help courts: in **Mohan Kumar Singhania (1991)** the Supreme Court said Article 51A can be used to interpret an ambiguous law and test its constitutionality, while in **Ramlila Maidan Incident (2012)** it noted that a common thread runs through Parts III, IV and IVA, and that any interpretation of one part should be judged against all three.","anchor":"significance"},{"topicId":"polity.fundamental-duties","topic":"Fundamental Duties","title":"Verma Committee and supporting laws","text":"The Verma Committee on Fundamental Duties of the Citizens (1999) catalogued existing laws that already back some duties.\n\n| Law | Duty it supports |\n|---|---|\n| Prevention of Insults to National Honour Act, 1971 | Respect for the Constitution, Flag and Anthem |\n| Criminal laws on promoting enmity between groups; A simple decision test helps. First ask whether the change concerns the Union–state balance or the structure of the courts and the President's election; if yes, state ratification is required in addition to the special majority. Next ask whether the subject is one of the routine, administrative items such as states' boundaries, citizenship, delimitation or the Union territories; if yes, an ordinary law passed by simple majority is enough and Article 368 is not even invoked. Everything else, including the whole of Parts III and IV, falls in the middle band and needs only the special majority of Parliament.\n\nRemember also that the two majorities used in the special route are measured differently. The first is counted against the House's whole strength, vacancies included, whereas the second is counted against the members actually present and voting. Both must be satisfied in each House separately.","anchor":"choosing-the-right-route-a-quick-method"},{"topicId":"polity.amendments","topic":"Amendment of the Constitution","title":"Revise in 60 seconds","text":"- Article 368, in Part XX, governs amendment of the Constitution by Parliament.\n- The Indian method is neither as flexible as Britain's nor as rigid as the USA's.\n- A bill can be introduced only in Parliament, by a minister or a private member, without the President's prior permission.\n- Each House must pass it separately by a majority of total membership and two-thirds of those present and voting.\n- There is no joint sitting for a deadlock on an amendment bill.\n- Federal subjects also need ratification by half of the state legislatures by simple majority, with no time limit.\n- The President must assent; this was made obligatory by the 24th Amendment (1971).\n- Three routes: simple majority, special majority, special majority with state ratification.\n- Fundamental Rights and Directive Principles are amended by special majority alone.\n- Kesavananda Bharati (1973): amendments cannot damage the basic structure.","anchor":"revise-in-60-seconds"},{"topicId":"polity.amendments","topic":"Amendment of the Constitution","title":"Common exam traps","text":"- A state legislature can introduce an amendment bill is incorrect; Singh government:** a fresh bill came in September 1990 after a chief ministers' conference, but lapsed with the government's fall.\n- **Narasimha Rao government:** a modified bill of September 1991 became the 73rd Amendment Act, 1992. Parliament passed it on 22–23 December 1992, 17 state assemblies ratified it, the President gave assent on 20 April 1993, and it came into force on 24 April 1993.\n\nThe Act inserted **Part IX, \"The Panchayats\"** (Articles 243 to 243-O) and the **Eleventh Schedule** with 29 subjects. It gives practical shape to Article 40, the Directive Principle that asks the State to organise village panchayats. Its provisions are of two kinds: **compulsory** ones that every state law must contain, and **voluntary** ones left to state discretion so that local conditions can be reflected.","anchor":"the-route-to-the-73rd-amendment"},{"topicId":"polity.panchayati-raj","topic":"Panchayati Raj","title":"Structure, elections and reservation","text":"$a1","anchor":"structure-elections-and-reservation"},{"topicId":"polity.panchayati-raj","topic":"Panchayati Raj","title":"Term, powers, finance and exclusions","text":"$a2","anchor":"term-powers-finance-and-exclusions"},{"topicId":"polity.panchayati-raj","topic":"Panchayati Raj","title":"Compulsory and voluntary provisions","text":"| Compulsory | Voluntary |\n|---|---|\n| Gram Sabha in a village or group of villages | Giving the Gram Sabha powers and functions |\n| Panchayats at village, intermediate and district levels | How the village chairperson is elected |\n| Direct election of all seats; indirect election of intermediate and district chairpersons | Representation of lower-tier chairpersons in the higher tier |\n| Voting rights for chairpersons and members | Representation of MPs and MLAs (both Houses) in panchayats of their area |\n| Minimum age 21 | Reservation for backward classes |\n| Reservation for SCs, STs and one-third for women, both for seats and chairpersons | Making panchayats self-governing institutions with devolved powers and the 29 functions |\n| Five-year term; election within six months after supersession | Financial powers: taxes, assigned revenues, grants, funds |\n| State Election Commission | |\n| State Finance Commission every five years | |","anchor":"compulsory-and-voluntary-provisions"},{"topicId":"polity.panchayati-raj","topic":"Panchayati Raj","title":"The Eleventh Schedule","text":"The Schedule lists 29 subjects, linked to Article 243G.
